The local detailed balance relation (27) together with (29) follows the same expression as (32), where however the identities of the dissipated energies $\Delta_r^+ Q(u)$ per reaction differ. Thus, (32) is more general in the sense that potentially accounts for chemical work through (31) in addition to enthalpy changes. A detailed mesoscopic derivation of (32), including multiple reservoirs, is given in Supp. Sec. S2.2.  

In Supp. Sec. S2.3 we review the consistency of (32) in the macroscopic limit for an ideal dilute solution, obeying mass action kinetics.  

Lastly, we note that beyond coupling a CRN to a chemostat with constant concentrations, more general time-dependent mechanisms can perturb the system away from equilibrium. Following the terminology of [70], these perturbations fall into two (not mutually exclusive) categories: (i) "Driving", which refers to coupling the system to an external agent that exchanges energy with it during state transitions, as in the case of chemostats; and (ii) "Manipulation", which involves time-dependent control of system parameters, such as changing chemostat concentrations or variations in the solution volume, for example due to cellular growth. Note that this terminology is not used consistently across the literature.  

In the presence of a time-varying protocol $\Gamma(t)$, the propensity functions become explicitly time-dependent, $\lambda_{r}^{\varepsilon}(u,t)$, resulting in a time-inhomogeneous Markov jump process. The structure of the chemical master equation (3) remains valid upon replacing the propensity functions with their time-dependent counterparts. Likewise, thermodynamic quantities such as $g(u,t)$ and $\mu_{l}(t)$ acquire explicit time dependence, yielding the generalized local detailed balance condition  

$$

\ln \left( \frac { \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( u , t ) } { \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } \left( u + v _ { r } , t \right) } \right) = \beta \; \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } Q ( u , t ) + \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } h _ { 1 } ( u , t )

$$  

together with the reaction-wise enthalpy balance relation  

$$

\Delta _ { r } ^ { \pm } Q ( u , t ) = \! W _ { \mathrm { c } } ^ { ( \pm r ) } ( t ) - \Delta _ { r } ^ { \pm } \mathfrak { h } ( u , t ) .

$$  

Eq. (33) follows by introducing the instantaneous stationary distribution $\pi(u,t)$, defined as the limiting distribution obtained by hypothetically freezing the protocol at $\Gamma(t)$ and allowing the system to relax. This distribution can then be used to derive (33) via the approach outlined in Supp. Sec. S2.2.  

This framework naturally extends to stochastic protocols, where the external control parameters are themselves governed by a stochastic process. In such cases, it is essential that the influence of the core system on the protocol, such as feedback on chemostat concentrations, remains negligible.  

# C. Thermodynamics of random trajectories  

A central appeal of stochastic thermodynamics is that quantities like heat dissipation, work, and entropy production can be defined along individual random trajectories. In the following, we review a priori definitions of these quantities, consistent with macroscopic thermodynamic principles. The presentation builds on Kurtz's process-based formulation of CRNs, which naturally accounts for the instantaneous, finite changes at the mesoscopic scale caused by chemical reactions of U.  

Let $0 < \tau_{1}^{(\pm r)} < \tau_{2}^{(\pm r)} < \cdots \leq t$ be the random times, when reaction $\Re_{r}$ happens in forward or backward direction.  

Heat is dissipated only during reaction events due to "excess" chemical work provided, even if the system is externally manipulated. The heat dissipated along the random trajectory $U_{[0,t]} := \{U(s)\}_{s\in[0,t]}$ of the open CRN within the time interval $(0,t]$ is then  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { q ( t ) = \underset { r = 1 } { \overset { | \mathcal { R } | } { \sum } } \left\{ \left( \underset { k : 0 < \tau _ { k } ^ { ( r ) } \leq t } { \sum } \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } Q ( U ( \tau _ { k } ^ { ( r ) } - ) , \tau _ { k } ^ { ( r ) } - ) \right) \right. } \\ { \left. + \left( \underset { k : 0 < \tau _ { k } ^ { ( - r ) } \leq t } { \sum } \Delta _ { r } ^ { - } Q ( U ( \tau _ { k } ^ { ( - r ) } - ) , \tau _ { k } ^ { ( - r ) } - ) \right) \right\} } \end{array}

$$  

where $U(t-) = \lim_{s \nearrow t} U(s)$ (or $U(t^-)$) denotes the left limit in time, such that $U(\tau_k^{(r)} -)$ is the state of the CRN just before the $k$-th occurrence of reaction $\mathfrak{R}_r$ in forward direction [63]. The left limit in the second argument of $\Delta_r^\pm Q$ is irrelevant if $\Gamma$ is a continuous function, but may be relevant for stochastic manipulation protocols. We restrict the discussion to deterministic protocols $\Gamma$ that are piecewise-differentiable and hence drop the left limit in the second argument.  

We rewrite (35) via stochastic integrals of the reaction counting processes  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { q ( t ) = \displaystyle \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } Q ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) \right. } \\ { \left. + \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Delta _ { r } ^ { - } Q ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) \right\} . } \end{array}

$$  

Defining the forward, backward, and net probability fluxes of reaction $\mathcal{R}_{r}$ as  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { J _ { r } ^ { + } ( u , t ) = \mathbb { P } ( U ( t ) = u ) \, \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( u , t ) , } \\ & { J _ { r } ^ { - } ( u , t ) = \mathbb { P } ( U ( t ) = u + v _ { r } ) \, \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } ( u + v _ { r } , t ) , \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } } \\ & { J _ { r } ( u , t ) = J _ { r } ^ { + } ( u , t ) - J _ { r } ^ { - } ( u , t ) , } \end{array}

$$  

the mean heat dissipation rate (also called thermal power [58]) is  

$$

\dot { Q } ( t ) = \sum _ { u } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } \, Q ( u ) \, J _ { r } ( u , t ) ,

$$  

as rigorously derived in Supp. Sec. S2.5.  

In a similar fashion, the chemical work performed on the trajectory $U_{[0,t]}$ is  

$$

w ^ { \mathrm { c } } ( t ) = \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } W _ { \mathrm { c } } ^ { ( r ) } ( s ) \big ( \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) - \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) \big ) ,

$$  

which gives rise to the mean rate of chemical work  

$$

\dot { W } _ { \mathrm { c } } ( t ) = \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \mathbb { E } [ w ^ { \mathrm { c } } ( t ) ] = \sum _ { u } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } W _ { \mathrm { c } } ^ { ( r ) } ( t ) J _ { r } ( u , t ) ,

$$  

by following the steps in the proof of (38). $\dot{W}_{\mathrm{c}}(t)$ can also be understood as the amount of energy required to maintain the chemostat concentrations, i.e., the energy expenditure of the external agent. In a manipulated system, the change of the free energy of the system amounts to another work contribution along $U_{[0,t]}$. For simplicity we assume that the internal energy is a continuous function of time, such that [70]  

$$

w ^ { \Gamma } ( t ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \partial _ { s } \mathfrak { h } ( U ( s ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } s .

$$  

and, hence, the mean rate of manipulated work is  

$$

\dot { W } _ { \Gamma } ( t ) = \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \mathbb { E } [ w ^ { \Gamma } ( t ) ] = \sum _ { u } p ( u , t ) \, \partial _ { t } \mathfrak { h } ( u , t ) .

$$  

The entire work, associated with manipulation and driving, is then  

$$

w ( t ) = w ^ { \Gamma } ( t ) + w ^ { \mathrm { c } } ( t ) .

$$  

In this account of work, we have ignored volume changes under isobaric conditions, since these are already accounted for in the enthalpy. The enthalpy change along the trajectory can be expressed via integrals, which account for both continuous evolution between reactions and changes due to reactions:  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \quad \mathfrak { h } ( U ( t ) , t ) - \mathfrak { h } ( U ( 0 ) , 0 ) } \\ & { = \! \! w ^ { \Gamma } ( t ) + \displaystyle \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \Bigg \{ \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } \mathfrak { h } ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) } \\ & { \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad + \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Delta _ { r } ^ { - } \, \mathfrak { h } ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) \Bigg \} . } \end{array}

$$  

and likewise also the internal entropy change  

$$

h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( t ) , t ) - h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( 0 ) , 0 ) = \beta q ^ { \mathrm { i } } ( t ) + \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \partial _ { s } h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( s ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } s .

$$  

where we denote the reactive change of the internal entropy as  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \beta q ^ { \mathrm { i } } ( t ) = \displaystyle \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) \right. } \\ { \left. + \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Delta _ { r } ^ { - } h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) \right\} . } \end{array}

$$  

Plug (34) into (36) and (41) into (40), which together yields the enthalpy balance relation  

$$

w ( t ) - q ( t ) = \mathfrak { h } ( U ( t ) , t ) - \mathfrak { h } ( U ( 0 ) , 0 ) ,

$$  

referred to as the first law of stochastic thermodynamics on the trajectory-level (cf. [60], Eq. (69)). Through the development of stochastic thermodynamics, this law was often reported in a different form, which is sometimes called "first law of stochastic thermodynamics" [70, 75]  

$$

w ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) - q ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) = g ( U ( t ) , t ) - g ( U ( 0 ) , 0 )

$$  

with $w_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{mes}}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}g(U(s),s)\,\mathrm{d}s$, $w_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{mes}}(t):=w_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{mes}}(t)+w^{\mathrm{c}}(t)$ and $q_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{mes}}(t):=q(t)+q^{\mathrm{i}}(t)$. It was first noted by Sekimoto [76] that the first-law-like equation was not consistent with the macroscopic first law, since the designated "mesoscopic heat exchange" $q_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{mes}}(t)$ contains an entropic part that does not account for heat flow, but a change in the system entropy. Since then, $q(t)$ is distinctly called measured heat [76] or calorimetric heat [70].  

We now turn two a trajectory-level account of the total entropy production of the open system. The total entropy production along $U_{[0,t]}$ comprises entropy changes of the system $\cup\cup\mathcal{S}$ and the entropy exchange with the environment:  

$$

h ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) = h ^ { \mathrm { s y s } } ( t ) + h ^ { \mathrm { e x } } ( t ) .

$$  

The system entropy itself contains two parts: (i) changes in the mesoscopic self-entropy  

$$

h _ { \mathrm { U } } ( u , s ) : = - \ln \big ( p \big ( U ( s ) , s \big ) \big ) \, ,

$$  

whose mean is simply the population (Shannon) entropy of the core system $H[U(s)] = \mathbb{E}[h_{\mathrm{U}}(U(s),s)] = \sum_{u}-p(U(s),s)\ln(p(U(s),s))$ at time $s \geq 0$, and (ii) changes in the internal entropy of the mesoscopic states $h_{\mathrm{i}}$. We denote the change of the population entropy along $U_{[0,t]}$ as  

$$

h ^ { \cup } ( t ) : = h _ { \cup } ( U ( t ) , t ) - h _ { \cup } ( U ( 0 ) , 0 ) = \ln \left( \frac { p ( U ( 0 ) , 0 ) } { p ( U ( t ) , t ) } \right) ,

$$  

and, in turn, define the system entropy as  

$$

h ^ { \mathrm { s y s } } ( t ) = h ^ { \cup } ( t ) + h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( t ) , t ) - h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( 0 ) , 0 ) .

$$  

Not much can be said about the change of internal entropy, but the population entropy is a quantity, which is given in terms of the kinetic model of the open CRN. Following an analogous decomposition as (42) we obtain  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { h ^ { \mathrm { U } } ( t ) = - \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \partial _ { s } \ln ( p ( U ( s ) , s ) ) \, \mathrm { d } s } \\ & { \qquad + \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { p ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) } { p ( U ( s ^ { - } ) + v _ { r } , s ) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) } \\ & { \qquad + \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { p ( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s ) } { p ( U ( s ^ { - } ) - v _ { r } , s ) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) , } \end{array}

$$  

where by the use of the CME (3) we may substitute  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \partial _ { s } \ln ( p ( U ( s ) , s ) ) \, \mathrm { d } s } \\ & { = \displaystyle \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Bigg \{ \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( U ( s ) - \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s ) \frac { p ( U ( s ) - \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s ) } { p ( U ( s ) , s ) } } \\ & { \qquad - \left( \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( U ( s ) , s ) + \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } ( U ( s ) , s ) \right) } \\ & { \qquad + \; \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } ( U ( s ) + \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s ) \frac { p ( U ( s ) + \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s ) } { p ( U ( s ) , s ) } \Bigg \} \, \mathrm { d } s . } \end{array}

$$  

We further have $\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t} \partial_{s} \ln(p(U(s),s))\,ds\right]=0$, which follows by pulling the expectation into the integral (Fubini's theorem) and applying a shift of summation. Using the CME (3) we can express the derivative of the mean population entropy as  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H [ U ( t ) ] = - \sum _ { u } ( \ln ( p ( u , t ) ) + 1 ) \, \partial _ { t } p ( u , t ) } \\ & { \qquad \qquad = \sum _ { u } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \ln \left( \frac { p ( u , t ) } { p ( u + v _ { r } , t ) } \right) J _ { r } ( u , t ) } \\ & { \qquad \qquad = \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \mathbb { E } \left[ h ^ { \cup } ( t ) \right] , } \end{array}

$$  

where the last equality follows from (44) and the proof method for Eq. (38) in Supp. Sec. S2.5.  

According to macroscopic thermodynamic principles [72], the heat dissipated into the environment $q(t)$ must be consistent with the increase of entropy in the environment $h^{\mathrm{ex}}$, such that  

$$

h ^ { \mathrm { e x } } ( t ) = \beta q ( t ) .

$$  

Now apply the generalized consistency relation (33) to the expression for the mesoscopic heat exchange (36), which yields  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \beta q ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) \! = \! \beta q ( t ) + \beta q ^ { \dot { \mathrm { i } } } ( t ) } \\ & { \qquad \qquad = \! \! \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) } { \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) + v _ { r } , s \right) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) \; \; \; \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \! \!

$$  

This representation shows that mesoscopic heat exchange captures all entropy flow associated with reaction events that is not accounted for by changes in the statistical distribution over mesostates or by external manipulation. As it depends explicitly on the propensity functions, it encodes the local directional bias of each reaction channel $\mathfrak{R}_r$, relating it to thermodynamic reversibility [77] as further discussed in Supp. Sec. S2.4.  

Again following the proof method for Eq. (38), the mean mesoscopic heat exchange rate (in units of $k_{\mathrm{B}}T$) satisfies  

$$

\beta \dot { Q } ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) = \sum _ { u } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \ln \left( \frac { \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( u , t ) } { \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } ( u + v _ { r } , t ) } \right) J _ { r } ( u , t ) ,

$$  

and thus depends only on the kinetic model of the open CRN. In conclusion, the total entropy production satisfies  

$$

h ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) = \underbrace { h ^ { \mathrm { U } } ( t ) + \beta q ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) } _ { \mathrm { p o p u l a t i o n ~ p a r t } } + \underbrace { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \partial _ { s } h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( U ( s ) , s ) \, \mathrm { d } s } _ { \mathrm { m i c r o s c o p i c ~ p a r t } } ,

$$  

which decomposes into a part that depends only on the time-dependent population distribution and on the propensity functions, and a term that accounts for internal entropic changes in the microscopic degrees of freedom due to external manipulation. The $\beta q^{1}$ terms cancel out.  

In the absence of external manipulation, e.g., when chemostat concentrations remain constant, the total entropy production of the open CRN depends solely on experimentally accessible quantities: the probability distribution over states and the reaction propensities. This key observation has already been emphasized before [63]. Using equations (44), (46), and (37), we obtain  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { h ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) \! = \! h ^ { \mathrm { U } } ( t ) + \beta q ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) } \\ & { \quad = \! \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \! \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \left\{ \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( U ( s ) - \nu _ { r } ) \frac { p ( U ( s ) - \nu _ { r } , s ) } { p ( U ( s ) , s ) } \right. } \\ & { \qquad \qquad \quad \left. - \left( \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } ( U ( s ) ) + \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } ( U ( s ) ) \right) \right. } \\ & { \qquad \qquad \quad \left. + \left. \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } ( U ( s ) + \nu _ { r } ) \frac { p ( U ( s ) + \nu _ { r } , s ) } { p ( U ( s ) , s ) } \right\} \, \mathrm { d } s \quad ( } \\ & { \qquad \qquad + \! \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \! \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { J _ { r } ^ { + } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) } { J _ { r } ^ { - } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) \right. } \\ & { \qquad \qquad \quad \left. + \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { J _ { r } ^ { - } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) - \nu _ { r } , s \right) } { J _ { r } ^ { + } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) - \nu _ { r } , s \right) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) \right\} , } \end{array}

$$  

where we used (44), (46) and (37). The (total) entropy production rate (EPR) then follows with $\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}\ln(p(U(s),s))\,\mathrm{d}s\right]=0$ and the method of proof of (38):  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) : = \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \mathbb { E } \left[ h ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) \right] } \\ & { \qquad \qquad = \sum _ { u } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \ln \left( \frac { J _ { r } ^ { + } \left( u , t \right) } { J _ { r } ^ { - } \left( u , t \right) } \right) J _ { r } ( u , t ) , } \end{array}

$$  

which can also be related to other mean rates  

$$

\frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) = \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H [ U ( t ) ] + \beta \dot { Q } ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) .

$$  

This decomposition motivated earlier inconsistent identifications of $\beta\dot{Q}^{\mathrm{mes}}$ with the time-dependent entropy exchange rate [62, 78].  

By virtue of $\ln(x)(x-1) \geq 0$ for all $x \geq 0$ (with convention $\ln(0) := \infty$), each term on the r.h.s. of (50) is non-negative and hence  

$$

\frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) \geq 0

$$  

shows consistency with the second law of macroscopic thermodynamics.  

# D. Energy dissipation and entropy production at stationarity  

Lastly, we look at the entropy production rate at stationarity, which in the following will be identified with the mean energy dissipation rate in a non-equilibrium steady state (NESS).  

At stationarity, we have $\lim_{t\to\infty}H[U(t)]=-\sum_u\pi(u)\ln(\pi(u))$, implying $\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}H[U(t)]=0$. Consequently, with (51) the entropy production rate at equilibrium or in a NESS satisfies  

$$

e _ { p } : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \to \infty } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \to \infty } \beta \dot { Q } ^ { \mathrm { m e s } } ( t ) .

$$  

The EPR at NESS hence quantifies the entropy changes in the whole system, consisting of $U \cup S$ and the environment, with all entropic changes that are associated with the dynamics of the CRN and not just changes in the population distribution.  

In a second step we note that stationarity of $U(t)$ together with the local equilibrium condition Supp. Eq. (S2.5) implies that also the process of all microscopic degrees of freedom $\Xi(t)$ is stationary (cf. Supp. Sec. S2.1). Then the mean rate of reactive changes of the internal entropy (43) also vanishes:  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \underset { t \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \mathbb { E } \left[ q ^ { \mathrm { i } } ( t ) \right] } \\ & { = \underset { t \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \mathbb { E } \left[ q ^ { \mathrm { i } } ( t ) \right] + \underset { t \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H [ U ( t ) ] } \\ & { = \underset { t \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H [ \Xi ( t ) ] = 0 . } \end{array}

$$  

Applying (46) then establishes the identification of the EPR with the energy dissipation rate (in units of $k_{\mathrm{B}}T$) at stationarity:  

$$

e _ { p } = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \to \infty } \dot { Q } ( t )

$$  

with mean heat exchange rate $\dot{Q}(t):=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[q(t)]$. Eq. (52) is key to the applications discussed in Sec. VI A and VI B.  

# E. Decompositions of heat dissipation and entropy production  

The central equality (52) motivates the definition of the so-called housekeeping heat [63, 79, 80] at trajectory level  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \quad \beta q ^ { \mathrm { h k } } ( t ) } \\ & { \quad : = \displaystyle \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) \pi \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) } { \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) + \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s \right) \pi \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) + \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s \right) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) } \\ & { \quad \quad + \displaystyle \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \left( \frac { \lambda _ { r } ^ { - } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) \pi \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) , s \right) } { \lambda _ { r } ^ { + } \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) - \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s \right) \pi \left( U ( s ^ { - } ) - \mathbf { v } _ { r } , s \right) } \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) . } \end{array}

$$  

where we again generalized to time-dependent protocols $\Gamma$, using the instantaneous stationary distribution $\pi(u,s)$. This quantity captures the portion of the total heat dissipation $q(t)$ along a trajectory $U_{[0,t]}$ that is required to maintain the instantaneous NESS of the system at any time $s \in [0,t]$. Notably, the definition remains valid even away from stationarity, allowing one to distinguish the excess heat, defined as $q^{\text{ex}}(t) = q(t) - q^{\text{hk}}(t)$, which quantifies the additional heat dissipated during relaxation toward the steady state.  

Similarly, there is the notion of adiabatic entropy production $h^{\mathrm{a}}(t)$ [80], which is also defined by the r.h.s. of (53), and is supposed to describes the entropy produced by an adiabatic protocol $\Gamma^{\mathrm{a}}$ that satisfies the ad-hoc assumption of instantaneous stationarity $p(\cdot,s)=\pi(\cdot,s)$ for all $s\in[0,t]$ and hence $\mathbb{E}[h^{\mathrm{tot}}(t)]=\mathbb{E}[h^{\mathrm{a}}(t)]$. In practice such a protocol is only realizable by a constant $\Gamma$ at stationarity [70], where even $h^{\mathrm{tot}}(t)=h^{\mathrm{a}}(t)$. The adiabatic entropy production allows for the decomposition of the total entropy production  

into an adiabatic term and the non-adiabatic entropy production $h^{\mathrm{na}}(t):=h^{\mathrm{tot}}(t)-h^{\mathrm{a}}(t)$. For a slowly manipulated closed system (i.e., without driving) following an adiabatic protocol, instantaneous detailed balance is approximately satisfied at all times. Using detailed balance as an ad-hoc assumption, yields $h^{\mathrm{a}}(t)=0$ and hence $h^{\mathrm{tot}}(t)=h^{\mathrm{na}}(t)$. While the intuition for this entropy production decomposition seems to be somewhat unripe, the two quantities exhibit important statistical properties [80].  

# F. Effective entropy production rate  

In practice, the chemostat species of an open CRN are often omitted when formulating the stoichiometric balance equations and the corresponding propensity functions (1). As (30) shows, this simplification does not necessarily affect the definition of the stochastic dynamics of the core process $U$. However, in the context of stochastic thermodynamics, it is essential to keep track of all species involved in each reaction. In particular, using such "effective" reactions leads to a systematic underestimation of the entropy production rate.  

In a properly defined open CRN it typically holds that $(\nu_{r_1},\gamma_{r_1})\neq(\nu_{r_2},\gamma_{r_2})$ and $(\nu_{r_1},\gamma_{r_1})\neq(-\nu_{r_2},-\gamma_{r_2})$ if $r_1\neq r_2$. However, the same changes in the core species U can be driven with different chemostat species.  

Definition 4. Two reaction channels $\mathfrak{R}_{r_1}$ and $\mathfrak{R}_{r_2}$ are called parallel, if $\nu_{r_1} = \nu_{r_2}$ and $\gamma_{r_1} \neq \gamma_{r_2}$, or $\nu_{r_1} = -\nu_{r_2}$ and $\gamma_{r_1} \neq -\gamma_{r_2}$.  

Let $\mathcal{C} := \{1, \ldots, |\mathcal{C}|\}$ with $|\mathcal{C}| \leq |\mathcal{R}|$ be the index set of the parallel reaction classes $\mathcal{C}_\alpha$. All reactions within a class be properly aligned such that $\nu_{r_1} = \nu_{r_2}$ for all $r_1, r_2 \in \mathcal{C}_\alpha$ with $r_1 \neq r_2$. Then we define effective reaction counters $\{\overline{R}_\alpha^\pm(t)\}_{t \geq 0}$ with effective propensity functions  

$$

\overline { { \lambda } } _ { \alpha } ^ { \pm } ( u ) : = \sum _ { r \in \mathcal { C } _ { \alpha } } \lambda _ { r } ^ { \pm } ( u )

$$  

and effective probability fluxes  

$$

\overline { { J } } _ { \alpha } ^ { \pm } ( u , t ) : = \sum _ { r \in \mathcal { C } _ { \alpha } } J _ { r } ^ { \pm } ( u , t ) = \overline { { \lambda } } _ { \alpha } ^ { \pm } ( u ) p ( u , t ) .

$$  

Applying (50) with these effective fluxes yields the "effective EPR" of the model with effective propensities:  

$$

\frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t , e f f } } ( t ) : = \sum _ { u } \sum _ { \alpha = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { C } | } \ln \left( \frac { \overline { { J } } _ { r } ^ { + } \left( u , t \right) } { \overline { { J } } _ { r } ^ { - } \left( u , t \right) } \right) \overline { { J } } _ { r } ( u , t ) ,

$$  

The effective EPR is a lower bound on the true EPR, i.e.,  

$$

0 \leq \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t , e f f } } ( t ) \leq \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) ,

$$  

as has been shown in [70] (cf. Eq. (3.81)), using the multi-reservoir description provided in the following section. We exemplify this inequality Sec. VIC with a two-state Markov model exhibiting two reaction channels. While in this case, $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}H^{\mathrm{tot,eff}}(t)=0$ for any choice of rate constants $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}H^{\mathrm{tot}}(t)$ can be positive.  

# G. Entropy Production Rate of driven conversion networks and general CTMCs with multiple reservoirs  

Much of the literature on entropy production rate employs a formulation based on the infinitesimal generator (transition rate matrix) $\Lambda$ of a CTMC, rather than a reaction network perspective [62, 70, 81]. A bridge between these two pictures can be constructed by introducing an index set $\mathcal{I} := \{1, \ldots, |\mathcal{U}|\}$, where each mesoscopic state $u \in \mathcal{U}$ is uniquely assigned an index $j \in \mathcal{I}$ (with $|\mathcal{U}| = \infty$ allowed). This indexing defines a one-hot encoding of the state space, $\mathcal{U}' = \{e_j: j \in \mathcal{I}\}$, where $e_j$ denotes the unit vector with a single non-zero entry at position $j$.  

Viewed as a population representation, this encoding interprets each state $e_j$ as indicating the presence of a single effective species $\mathbf{U}_j'$, with all other species absent. This reformulation recasts the original reaction network into a conversion network: a collection of microscopically reversible transitions between discrete one-hot states. Each conversion $i \rightarrow j$ (with change vector $\mathbf{v}_{(i,j)} = e_j - e_i$) may be coupled to one or more reservoirs $r$, corresponding to the reaction channels $\mathfrak{R}_r$, as discussed in Supp. Sec. S2.2.  

For each reservoir $r$, we denote the set of reversible transitions it mediates as  

$$

\mathcal { R } ^ { ( r ) } : = \{ ( i , j ) \in \mathcal { J } ^ { 2 } : i < j , \; \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( + r ) } ( e _ { i } ) > 0 \} .

$$  

with stoichiometric balance equations  

$$

\mathfrak { R } _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( r ) } : \quad \bigcup _ { i } \underset { \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( - r ) } } { \overset { \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( + r ) } } { \rightleftharpoons } } \bigcup _ { j } , \quad \mathrm { w i t h ~ } ( i , j ) \in \mathcal { R } ^ { ( r ) }

$$  

and reservoir-specific propensity functions $\lambda_{(i,j)}^{(\pm r)}(u')$.  

The effective Markov generator $\Lambda$ of such a network, coupled to multiple reservoirs $r$ is then given as the sum of reservoir-wise generators $\Lambda^{(r)}$, such that the transition rates for $i \rightarrow j$ (and also diagonal elements) satisfy [82]  

$$

\Lambda _ { i j } = \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \Lambda _ { i j } ^ { ( r ) } .

$$  

For each $(i,j) \in \mathcal{R}^{(r)}$ the reservoir-wise propensity functions and generators are related as  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( + r ) } ( u ^ { \prime } ) = \Lambda _ { i j } ^ { ( r ) } \mathbb { 1 } \{ u ^ { \prime } = e _ { i } \} , } \\ { \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( - r ) } ( u ^ { \prime } ) = \Lambda _ { j i } ^ { ( r ) } \mathbb { 1 } \{ u ^ { \prime } = e _ { j } \} . } \end{array}

$$  

The reservoir-wise generators then satisfy the local detailed balance relation for $(i,j) \in \mathcal{R}^{(r)}$ (cf. Supp. Sec. S2.2 and [82], Eq. (4))  

$$

\ln \left( \frac { \Lambda _ { i j } ^ { ( r ) } } { \Lambda _ { j i } ^ { ( r ) } } \right) = \ln \left( \frac { \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( + r ) } ( e _ { i } ) } { \lambda _ { ( i , j ) } ^ { ( - r ) } ( e _ { j } ) } \right) = \beta \; \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } Q ( e _ { i } ) + \Delta _ { r } ^ { + } h _ { \mathrm { i } } ( e _ { i } ) .

$$  

By (50) we obtain  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) = \sum _ { i \in \mathcal { I } } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \sum _ { ( k , j ) \in \mathcal { R } ^ { ( r ) } } \ln \left( \frac { p ( e _ { i } , t ) \, \lambda _ { ( k , j ) } ^ { ( + r ) } ( e _ { k } ) } { p ( e _ { i } + e _ { j } - e _ { k } , t ) \, \lambda _ { ( k , j ) } ^ { ( - r ) } ( e _ { j } ) } \right) } \\ { \times \left( \lambda _ { ( k , j ) } ^ { ( + r ) } ( e _ { k } ) p ( e _ { i } , t ) - \lambda _ { ( k , j ) } ^ { ( - r ) } ( e _ { j } ) p ( e _ { i } + e _ { j } - e _ { k } , t ) \right) } \end{array}

$$  

and hence substituting (54) yields the multi-reservoir EPR of a consistently modeled CTMC [82]  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } H ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } ( t ) = \sum _ { i \in \mathcal { I } } \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { | \mathcal { R } | } \sum _ { j > i } \ln \left( \frac { p ( e _ { i } , t ) \, \Lambda _ { i j } ^ { ( r ) } } { p ( e _ { j } , t ) \, \Lambda _ { j i } ^ { ( r ) } } \right) } \\ { \times \, ( \Lambda _ { i j } ^ { ( r ) } \, p ( e _ { i } , t ) - \Lambda _ { j i } ^ { ( r ) } \, p ( e _ { j } , t ) ) . } \end{array}

$$  

Importantly, neglecting the contribution of individual reservoirs can result in an underestimation of the EPR (cf. Ref. 70, Eq. (3.81)).  

# V. SPECIES-REACTION GRAPHS AND LOCAL INDEPENDENCE GRAPHS FOR CRNS  

# A. Species reaction graphs  

We define a species-reaction graph (SR-graph) slightly different from its original notion [83]. This is necessary to faithfully represent local independence. A reversible SR-graph is a bipartite graph ($\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{R}, \mathcal{E}$) with edge set $\mathcal{E}$ and two vertex types: (i) the species nodes $\mathcal{S}$ for all $\cup_{d}$, and (ii) the reversible reaction channel nodes $\mathcal{R}$ for all reaction channels $\Re_{r}$. Here, the SR-graph attribute "reversible" means that we do not resolve forward and backward direction of a single reaction channel $\Re_{r}$ if the reaction channel is reversible. However, the reversible SR-graph may actually comprise irreversible reaction channels. In contrast, we refer to an SR-graph as irreversible, if all reaction channels are considered irreversible.  

In a bipartite graph, only vertices of different type can be connected by an edge $e \in \mathcal{E}$, i.e.,  

$$

\mathcal { E } \subseteq ( \mathcal { I } \times \mathcal { R } ) \cup ( \mathcal { R } \times \mathcal { I } ) ,

$$  

where we assume distinct enumeration $\mathcal{I} \cap \mathcal{R} = \emptyset$ for notational simplicity. A directed edge $(d,r) \in \mathcal{I} \times \mathcal{R}$ is drawn from $\mathbb{U}_d$ to $\Re_r$ if either $\lambda_r^+$ or $\lambda_r^-$ explicitly depends on $u_d$. Such edges encapsulate local (in)dependencies of the reaction counters. A directed edge $(r,d) \in \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{I}$ is drawn from $\Re_r$ to $\mathbb{U}_d$ if $v_r$ is non-zero in the coordinate of $\mathbb{U}_d$. These edges encapsulate the time-point wise algebraic equation  

$$

U _ { d } ( s ) = U _ { d } ( 0 ) + \sum _ { r \in \mathcal { R } ^ { d } } \pi _ { d } ( \mathbf { v } _ { r } ) \left( R _ { r } ^ { + } ( s ) - R _ { r } ^ { - } ( s ) \right) ,

$$  

where $\pi_{d}$ is the coordinate map to $d \in \mathcal{S}$ and $\mathcal{R}^{d} := \{r \in \mathcal{R} \mid \pi_{d}(v_{r}) \neq 0\}$. If directed edges in both directions exist, they can be replaced by a single bidirected edge “$\longleftrightarrow$” in the visualization of the graph.  

An edge-simple walk (often called a 'trail' in classical graph theory [84]) of length $N \in \mathbb{N}$, in the SR-graph is sequence of directed edges $\vec{e} = (e_1, \ldots, e_N)$ which joins a sequence of vertices $(v_0, \ldots, v_N)$, such that all directed edges $e_k = (v_{k-1}, v_k)$ are pairwise distinct. A edge-simple walk $\vec{e}$ is said to be from $v_0$ to $v_N$. We say that $\vec{e}$ passes through some vertex $v$, if exists $i \in \{1, \ldots, N-1\}$ such that $v = v_i$. For edge-simple walks between sets instead of single vertices we use the following definition.  

Definition 5. Let $\mathcal{R}^1 \subseteq \mathcal{R}$ and consider the disjoint species sets $\mathcal{S}_X, \mathcal{S}_Y \subset \mathcal{S}$. An edge-simple walk of length $N \in \mathbb{N}$ is said to be  

(a) from $\mathcal{S}_X$ to $\mathcal{S}_Y$ if $v_0 \in \mathcal{S}_X$, $v_N \in \mathcal{S}_Y$ and $v_1, \ldots, v_{N-1} \notin \mathcal{S}_X \cup \mathcal{S}_Y$.  

(b) from $\mathcal{R}'$ to $\mathcal{S}_Y$ if $v_0 \in \mathcal{R}'$, $v_N \in \mathcal{S}_Y$ and $v_1, \ldots, v_{N-1} \notin \mathcal{R}' \cup \mathcal{S}_Y$.  

By this definition, a 'trail' from $\mathcal{I}_X$ to $\mathcal{I}_Y$ is always a 'trail' on the edge-deleted subgraph $(\mathcal{I}, \mathcal{R}, \mathcal{E}_{X\to Y})$ with  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } { \mathcal { E } _ { X \to Y } : = \mathcal { E } \backslash \big ( \{ ( d , r ) \in \mathcal { S } \times \mathcal { R } \mid d \in \mathcal { S } _ { Y } \} } & { { } } \\ { \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad

$$  

which does neither contain edges into $\mathscr{I}_X$ nor edges that originate from $\mathscr{I}_Y$. Similarly, a 'trail' from $\mathscr{R}'$ to $\mathscr{I}_Y$ is a 'trail' on the edge-deleted subgraph $(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{R},\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{R}'\to Y})$ with  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } { \mathcal { E } _ { \mathcal { R } ^ { \prime } \rightarrow Y } : = \mathcal { E } \backslash \left( \{ ( d , r ) \in \mathcal { S } \times \mathcal { R } \mid d \in \mathcal { S } _ { Y } \} \right. } & { } \\ { \left. \cup \{ ( d , r ) \in \mathcal { S } \times \mathcal { R } \mid r \in \mathcal { R } ^ { \prime } \} \right) . } & { } \end{array}

$$  

# B. Local independence graphs for multivariate counting processes  

We briefly introduce the concepts of local independence graphs and dynamic Markov properties for multivariate counting processes, as developed by Didelez [85].  

Temporarily assume that $\mathcal{R}$ is a set of irreversible reaction indices. We introduce bold notation for the multivariate counting process $\boldsymbol{R}:=(R_{r}:r\in\mathcal{R})$. For any $A\subseteq\mathcal{R}$ denote the subprocess $\boldsymbol{R}^{A}:=(R_{r}:r\in A)$ and its internal filtration $\mathcal{F}_{s}^{A}:=\bigvee_{r\in A}\sigma(R_{r}(\tau):\tau\leq s)$. Further let $s\rightarrow\kappa_{s}(r)$ denote the $(\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})$-intensity process of $R_{r}$. Thereby $\kappa_{s}(r)$ may depend on the entire past $\mathcal{F}_{s-}$, i.e., $\boldsymbol{R}$ may be a non-Markovian process. The process $(\boldsymbol{R},\mathbb{P})$ is always associated with a probability measure, which determines its intensity processes. Further, it is also associated with a finite observation interval $[0,t]$, $t>0$. In the following, we simply write $\boldsymbol{R}$ unless required.  

Definition 6 ([85]). Let $A, B, C \subseteq \mathcal{R}$ be pairwise disjoint. We say that a subprocess $\mathbf{R}^{B}$ is locally independent of $\mathbf{R}^{A}$ given $\mathbf{R}^{C}$ over $[0, t]$ if for all $r \in B$ the $\mathcal{F}^{A \cup B \cup C}$-intensities $\tilde{\lambda}_{s}(r) := \mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{s}(r) \mid \mathcal{F}_{s-}^{A \cup B \cup C}\right]$ are $\mathcal{F}^{B \cup C}$-intensities. This is denoted by $A \nrightarrow B \mid C$. Otherwise, $\mathbf{R}^{B}$ is locally dependent on $\mathbf{R}^{A}$ given $\mathbf{R}^{C}$, i.e., $A \rightarrow B \mid C$. If $C = \emptyset$, then $\mathbf{R}^{B}$ is marginally locally (in)dependent of $\mathbf{R}^{A}$.  

Although this is not necessary in general, the above definition assumes that local independence is "reflexive", i.e., each subprocess depends at least on its own past, which is the case for most practical applications. $A, B, C \subseteq \mathcal{R}$, not necessarily disjoint,  

Now let $\mathcal{G}:=(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})$ be a directed graph (possibly containing cycles) with directed edge set  

$$

\mathcal { A } \subseteq \{ ( r ^ { \prime } , r ) \in \mathcal { R } \times \mathcal { R } \mid r ^ { \prime } \neq r \} .

$$  

Definition 7 ([85]). $\mathcal{G}$ is called local independence graph of $R$ if $R$ satisfies the pairwise dynamic Markov property with respect to $\mathcal{G}$, i.e., for all $r', r \in \mathcal{R}$, $r' \neq r$  

$$

( r ^ { \prime } , r ) \notin \mathcal { A } \quad \implies \quad \{ r ^ { \prime } \} \not \to \{ r \} \mid \mathcal { R } \backslash \{ r ^ { \prime } , r \} .

$$  

In the following, we need a few more notions of graph theory [86] and will mainly adhere to the conventions of Didelez [87] (cf. Sec. 1.1). To keep these notions abstract, we temporarily denote the finite vertex set as $\mathcal{V}$ and the edge set as $\mathcal{E}$, where the edge set may contain both directed and undirected edges (denoted as sets), i.e.,  

$$

\mathcal { E } \subseteq \left\{ ( v ^ { \prime } , v ) \in \mathcal { V } ^ { 2 } \, | \, v ^ { \prime } \neq v \right\} \cup \left\{ \{ v ^ { \prime } , v \} \subseteq \mathcal { V } \, | \, v ^ { \prime } \neq v \right\} .

$$  

We consider only reciprocal graphs, where $\{v',v\}\in\mathcal{E}$ implies $(v',v),(v,v')\notin\mathcal{E}$. Let $\mathcal{G}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})$ denote the graph. For $A\subset\mathcal{V}$ we call $\mathcal{G}_A:=(A,\mathcal{E}|_A)$ the induced subgraph, where $\mathcal{E}|_A:=\mathcal{E}\cap((A\times A)\cup\{\{v',v\}\subseteq A\})$. For $B\subset\mathcal{V}$ we denote with $\mathcal{G}^B:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^B)$ the edge-deleted subgraph with  

$$

\mathcal { E } ^ { B } : = \mathcal { E } \setminus \left\{ ( v , v ^ { \prime } ) \in \mathcal { E } \mid v \in B \right\} .

$$  

A tuple $\vec{v}=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N})$ of distinct vertices $v_{k}\in\mathcal{V}$ (except possibly $v_{0}=v_{N}$) is called a path of length $N\in\mathbb{N}$ from $v_{0}$ to $v_{N}$ if $\{v_{k-1},v_{k}\}\in\mathcal{E}$ or $(v_{k-1},v_{k})\in\mathcal{E}$ for all $k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}$. A path $\vec{v}$ is called semidirected if exists $k$ with $(v_{k-1},v_{k})\in\mathcal{E}$ and directed the latter holds for all $k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}$. Note that Didelez [87] also introduced a generalized notion of trails for the given class of graphs [88]. A chain graph is a (reciprocal) graph that does not contain any (semi)directed cycles.  

Let $a,b\in\mathcal{V}$, $a\neq b$ and $A,B,C\subset\mathcal{V}$. A set $C$ is called an $(a,b)$-separator if all paths from $a$ to $b$ intersect $C$. We say that $C$ separates $A$ from $B$ if $C$ is an $(a,b)$-separator for all $a\in A$ and $b\in B$. $a$ is a parent of $b$ ($a\in\text{pa}(b)$) if $(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}$ and $\text{pa}(A):=\cup_{v\in A}\text{pa}(v)\setminus A$. $b$ is a child of $a$ ($b\in\text{ch}(a)$) if $(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}$ and $\text{ch}(A):=\cup_{v\in A}\text{ch}(v)\setminus A$. $a$ is a neighbor of/adjacent to $b$ ($a\in\text{nb}(b)$) if $\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{E}$ and $\text{nb}(A)=\cup_{v\in A}\text{nb}(v)\setminus A$. $a$ and $b$ are non-adjacent if $\{(a,b),(b,a),\{a,b\}\}\cap\mathcal{E}=\emptyset$. The union of parents and neighbors is called the boundary $\text{bd}(A)=\text{pa}(A)\cup\text{nb}(A)$ and $\text{cl}(A):=\text{bd}(A)\cup A$ is the closure of $A$. If there is a path from $a$ to $b$, then $a$ is an ancestor of $b$ ($a\in\text{an}(b)$) and $b$ is a descendant of $a$ ($b\in\text{de}(a)$). For sets $\text{an}(A):=\cup_{v\in A}\text{an}(v)\setminus A$, $\text{de}(A):=\cup_{v\in A}\text{de}(v)\setminus A$, and additionally the non-descendants $\text{nd}(A):=\mathcal{V}\setminus(\text{de}(A)\cup A)$. A set $A$ is called ancestral if $\text{bd}(A)=\emptyset$. The smallest ancestral set, containing $A$ is denoted as $\text{An}(A)$ and satisfies $\text{An}(A)=\text{an}(A)\cup A$.  

The moralized graph $\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{m}}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{m}})$ is an undirected graph, such that $\{v',v\}\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{m}}$ if $(v',v)\in\mathcal{E}$ or $(v,v')\in\mathcal{E}$, or  

if exists $\tilde{v} \in \mathcal{V}$ such that $\{v', v\} \subseteq \text{pa}_{\mathcal{G}}(\Upsilon(\tilde{v}))$, where $\Upsilon(\tilde{v}) := \{v \in \mathcal{V} \mid \exists \text{ undirected path form } \tilde{v} \text{ to } v\}$ and $\text{pa}_{\mathcal{G}}$ means "parent with respect to graph $\mathcal{G}$".  

With these notions, and switching back to the graph $\mathcal{G} = (\mathcal{R}, \mathcal{A})$, an asymmetric separation criterion can be defined.  

Definition 8 ([85]). For pairwise disjoint $A, B, C \subset \mathcal{R}$ we say that $C$ $\delta$-separates $A$ from $B$ in $\mathcal{G}$ if $C$ separates $A$ from $B$ in the undirected graph $(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{An}(A \cup B \cup C)}^{\mathrm{B}})^{\mathrm{m}}$.  

For $A,B,C\subset\mathcal{R}$ not pairwise disjoint we say that $C$ $\delta$-separates $A$ from $B$ if $C\setminus B$ $\delta$-separates $A\setminus(B\cup C)$ from $B$. Further, by convention $\emptyset$ is always $\delta$-separated from $B$ and $\emptyset$ $\delta$-separates $A$ from $B$ if $A$ and $B$ are unconnected in $(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{An}(A\cup B)}^{B})^{\mathrm{m}}$.  

Note that the moralization criterion in the above definition applies only in the disjoint case. With the given graph theory notions two further dynamic Markov properties can be defined.  

Definition 9 ([85]). $\mathbf{R}$ is said to satisfy the local dynamic Markov property with respect to $\mathcal{G}$ if for all $r \in \mathcal{R}$  

$$

\mathcal { R } \setminus \mathrm { c l } ( r ) \nrightarrow \{ r \} \mid \mathrm { p a } ( r ) ,

$$  

i.e., the $\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{R}}$-intensity $\bar{\lambda}(r)$ is a $\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cl}(r)}$-intensity.  

$\mathbf{R}$ is said to satisfy the global dynamic Markov property with respect to $\mathcal{G}$ if for all pairwise disjoint $A, B, C \subseteq \mathcal{R}$  

$$

C \; \delta { \mathrm { - s e p a r a t e s } } \; A \; { \mathrm { f r o m } } \; B \quad \implies \quad A \not \to B \mid C .

$$  

Note that the generalization of the global dynamic Markov property to the non-disjoint case is straightforward. Didelez proved two theorems – a criterion for (symmetric) conditional independence and a criterion for the equivalence of all dynamic Markov properties – which we restate in the following.  

Proposition 1 ([85, 89]). Let $A, B, C \subseteq \mathcal{R}$ be pairwise disjoint.  

(a) Let $\mathcal{G}$ be the local independence graph of $\mathbf{R}$. If $C$ separates $A$ from $B$ on $(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{An}(A\cup B\cup C)})^{\mathrm{m}}$, then  

$$

\mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { A } \perp \! \! \! \perp \mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { B } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { C } \quad f o r \; a l l \; s \in [ 0 , t ] .

$$  

(b) If $\mathbf{R}$ satisfies conditional measurable separability [90], i.e., for all $D,E \subseteq \mathcal{R}$ and all $s \in [0,t]$  

$$

\overline { { \mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { D } } } \cap \overline { { \mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { E } } } = \overline { { \mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { D \cap E } } }

$$  

with convention $\mathcal{F}^{\emptyset}:=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}$, then (under mild regularity conditions) all dynamic Markov properties are equivalent.  

Hence, given that $\mathcal{G}$ is a local independence graph of $R$, Proposition 1 allows the use of graphical separation criteria to obtain "global" local independence relations. Further, (a) has several implications: (i) $A \nrightarrow B \mid C$, (ii) $B \nrightarrow A \mid C$, and (iii) for each $r \in C$ the $\mathcal{F}^{A \cup B \cup C}$-intensity $\tilde{\lambda}(r)$ is either a $\mathcal{F}^{A \cup C}$- or a $\mathcal{F}^{B \cup C}$-intensity. The application of local independence graphs to the reaction counters of CRNs requires the extension with a non-dynamic node for each species in $\mathcal{I}$, which  

jointly represent the initial state $U(0)$. In general, local independence graphs can be extended with time-fixed covariates, whose information must then be included in the filtration at the start $\mathcal{F}_0$. The subgraph induced by non-dynamic nodes must be either a directed acyclic graph or a chain graph [85]. Edges from dynamic to non-dynamic nodes are not allowed. This construction ensures that the induced subgraph of non-dynamic nodes can faithfully represent conditional independence relations and (non-dynamic) Markov properties. In the following, we formalize  

Let $\mathcal{I}$ be the set of $\mathcal{F}_0$-measurable baseline covariates $\theta_i$, $i \in \mathcal{I}$, such that $\mathcal{I} \cap \mathcal{R} = \emptyset$. These covariates can, for example, be (random) initial conditions or time-dependent parametrizations of intensity processes, which are fully determined at time zero. Again, we introduce a multivariate random variable on a subset $I \subseteq \mathcal{I}$ with notation $\boldsymbol{\theta}^I := \{\theta_i: i \in I\}$. As before let $\mathcal{R}$ be the set of counting processes. We define the extended graph $\mathcal{G} := (\mathcal{I} \cup \mathcal{R}; \mathcal{B}, \mathcal{A})$ with the set of undirected edges between baseline covariates  

$$

\mathcal { B } \subseteq \{ \{ i , i ^ { \prime } \} \subseteq \mathcal { I } \mid i \neq i ^ { \prime } \}

$$  

and directed edges  

$$

\mathcal { A } \subseteq \{ ( j , j ^ { \prime } ) \in ( \mathcal { I } \cup \mathcal { R } ) ^ { 2 } : j \neq j ^ { \prime } , \{ j , j ^ { \prime } \} \notin \mathcal { B } \}

$$  

with $\mathcal{A} \cap (\mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{I}) = \emptyset$. The induced subgraph $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}$ be a chain graph. The graph $\mathcal{G}$ is equivalent to a dynamic graph [87]. For any set $G \subseteq \mathcal{I} \cup \mathcal{R}$ we assign the subfiltration $\mathcal{F}^{G}$ such that for all $s \in [0, t]$  

$$

\mathcal { F } _ { s } ^ { G } : = \left( \bigvee _ { r \in G \cap \mathcal { R } } \sigma ( R _ { r } ( \tau ) \colon \tau \in [ 0 , s ] ) \right) \vee \left( \bigvee _ { i \in G \cap \mathcal { I } } \sigma ( \theta _ { i } ) \right) .

$$  

On the extended local independence graph $\mathcal{G}$ we need to generalize the notion of local independence and Markov properties. In particular, dynamic properties can only be defined for subsets of $\mathcal{R}$. Definition 6 (local independence) and 8 ($\delta$-separations) and all dynamic Markov properties can be readily generalized for $A, C \subseteq \mathcal{I} \cup \mathcal{R}$ and $B \subseteq \mathcal{R}$ pairwise disjoint, using the extended subfiltrations.  

Definition 10. $\mathbf{R}$ is said to satisfy the local dynamic Markov property with respect to $\mathcal{G}$ if for all $r \in \mathcal{R}$  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { ( \mathcal { I } \cup \mathcal { R } ) \setminus \mathrm { c l } ( r ) \not \to \{ r \} \mid \mathrm { p a } ( r ) , } \end{array}

$$  

i.e., the $\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{R}}$-intensity $\bar{\lambda}(r)$ is a $\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cl}(r)}$-intensity.  

R is said to satisfy the global dynamic Markov property with respect to $\mathcal{G}$ if for all A, C $\subseteq$ $\mathcal{I} \cup \mathcal{R}$ and B $\subseteq$ $\mathcal{R}$, pairwise disjoint,  

$$

C \; \delta { \mathrm { - s e p a r a t e s } } \; A \; f r o m \; B \quad \implies \quad A \not \rightarrow B \mid C .

$$  

As one of our key results, we show that local independence is equivalent to a vanishing causally conditioned directed information. The proof is provided in Supp. Sec. S1.4.  

Theorem 3. Let $A, C \subseteq \mathcal{I} \cup \mathcal{R}$ and $B \subseteq \mathcal{R}$ be pairwise disjoint. Then $A \nleftrightarrow B \mid C$ if and only if  

$$

\mathbb { I } \left( \pmb { \theta } ^ { A \cap \mathcal { I } } , \pmb { R } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { A \cap \mathcal { R } } \rightarrow \pmb { R } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { B } \Vert \pmb { \theta } ^ { C \cap \mathcal { I } } , \pmb { R } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { C \cap \mathcal { R } } \right) = 0 .

$$  

We conclude by providing an extension of Definition 7, which is sufficient for our purposes.  

Definition 11. $\mathcal{G}$ is called an (extended) local independence graph of $(\theta, R, \mathbb{P})$ if the following is satisfied:  

(i) $\mathbb{P} \circ \boldsymbol{\theta}^{-1}$ satisfies the pairwise chain Markov property [86], relative to $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}$, i.e., for any pair $i, i'$ of non-adjacent vertices with $i' \in \text{nd}(i)$ satisfies $i \perp_{\mathcal{J}} i' \mid \text{nd}(i) \setminus \{i'\}$;  

(ii) For all $j \in \mathcal{J} \cup \mathcal{R}$, $r \in \mathcal{R}$, $j \neq r$ it holds that  

$$

( j , r ) \notin \mathcal { A } \quad \implies \quad \{ j \} \nrightarrow \{ r \} \mid ( \mathcal { I } \cup \mathcal { R } ) \backslash \{ j , r \} .

$$  

Given that all deterministic parameters are fixed (and hence do not appear as nodes in the local independence graph), we assume conditional measurable separability for every relevant case in this paper. Hence, we always have equivalence of dynamic Markov properties for the generalized case with random baseline covariates.  

# C. Local independence graphs for Markovian CRNs  

We have thus far claimed that the SR-graph of the Markovian CRN $U$ faithfully represents local (in)dependencies. Now we derive the extended local independence graph of $(U(0),\boldsymbol{R})$ from the "reversible" SR-graph. Here, any process node $r\in\mathcal{R}$ represents the collection $\boldsymbol{R}^{\{r\}}=(R_{r}^{+},R_{r}^{-})$ if the reaction channel $\mathfrak{R}_{r}$ is reversible and $\boldsymbol{R}^{\{r\}}=R_{r}^{+}$ otherwise. For simplicity of notation, assume distinct enumeration of species and reactions, i.e., $\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset$. The local independence graph of the reactions of a CRN is complementary to the related concept of a kinetic independence graph [91]. The latter is formulated on the level of species instead of reactions.  

In the following, let the subscript 0 for parents, children etc. stand for the respective functions with respect to the SR-graph and not the local independence graph. That is, for any $A \subseteq \mathcal{B}$, $\text{pa}_0(A) \subset \mathcal{S}$ are the set of species that modulate reactions in $A$ and $\text{ch}_0(A) \subset \mathcal{S}$ are the set of species changed by reactions in $A$. We construct the edge sets $\mathcal{A}$ and $\mathcal{B}$ of the graph  

$$

\mathcal { G } = \left( \mathcal { S } \cup \mathcal { R } ; \mathcal { B } , \mathcal { A } \right)

$$  

from the edge set $\mathcal{E}$ by iteratively adding elements. For each $r \in \mathcal{R}$ add to $\mathcal{A}$ the in-arrows from its parent's parents, ignoring reflexive edges. To account for the dependence of reaction counters on the initial copy numbers of species $\mathcal{I}$, add directed edges from the parents. Consequently,  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { A } = \left\{ ( r ^ { \prime } , r ) \in \mathcal { R } ^ { 2 } \mid r ^ { \prime } \in \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( r ) ) \setminus \{ r \} \right\} } \\ & { \qquad \cup \left\{ ( d , r ) \in \mathcal { S } \times \mathcal { R } \mid d \in \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( r ) \right\} . } \end{array}

$$  

Further, we assume the most general case of dependences between initial copy numbers of all species by assuming that $\mathcal{I}$, the covariate set of the graph, is a complete subset relative to $\mathcal{G}$, i.e., every pair of vertices on the subspace is joined by an undirected edge, such that  

$$

\mathcal { B } = \left\{ \left\{ d , d ^ { \prime } \right\} \subset \mathcal { S } \mid d \neq d ^ { \prime } \right\} .

$$  

We verify that $\mathcal{G}$ is a local independence graph in accordance with Def. 11. The pairwise chain Markov property, relative to $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}$ is hence trivially satisfied due to completeness. Next, consider any $(j,r) \notin \mathcal{A}$. By construction we have  

$$

j \notin \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( r ) ) \setminus \{ r \} , } & { \mathrm { i f ~ } j \in \mathcal { R } } \\ { \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( r ) , } & { \mathrm { i f ~ } j \in \mathcal { S } . } \end{array} \right.

$$  

Hence, if $j \in \mathcal{R}$, no species that is changed by $j$ modulates $r$ (i.e., $\text{ch}_0(j) \cap \text{pa}_0(r) = \emptyset$), and if $j \in \mathcal{S}$, it does not modulate $r$ since $j \notin \text{pa}_0(r)$. Consequently, $\{j\} \not\to \{r\} \mid (\mathcal{S} \cup \mathcal{R}) \setminus \{j, r\}$, i.e., the pairwise dynamic Markov property is satisfied.  

# VI. DISCUSSION OF COMBINED APPLICATIONS  

We explore two application areas, namely continuous-time communication systems and and optimal design principles, where continuous-time path mutual information and directed information are combined with stochastic thermodynamic constraints. Other research traditions also merge information theory with thermodynamics, most notably information thermodynamics and information dynamics. A detailed comparison with these literatures lies outside the scope of the present work; for a comparison with the concept of learning rates, see Chetrite and Gupta [16], and for related contributions in information dynamics, see Spinney et al. [92].  

We first adopt a classical information-theoretic viewpoint by interpreting deterministically manipulated chemical reaction networks as communication channels, where information encoding and transmission of information entail a thermodynamic cost in the form of heat dissipation, entropy production or work. Then we discuss the utility of the combined frameworks in evaluating the hypothesis that information flow functions as an emergent optimization principle, implicitly followed by natural selection in biological evolution [6, 93, 94]. As a complementary perspective, we also emphasize the relevance for synthetic biology, where these tools can inform the design of chemical reaction networks that are optimized for energy-efficient information processing.  

In general, one should be careful with continuous approximations of CRNs, such as the chemical Langevin equation, in both information theory and thermodynamics. While heat dissipation may still be well approximated by diffusion models, stochastic consistency (cf. Supp. Sec. S2.4) is typically lost [77]. In a similar way, path-information measures are not consistent under continuous approximations because the continuum limit eliminates "discrete reaction information" [35].  

# A. Chemical reaction networks as communication channels  

# 1. A causal chemical communication model  

We introduce the communication model of interest, closely following the marked Poisson-type channel described by Frey [24]. Let $t > 0$ be the transmission duration and $M: \Omega \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ be a random variable, $\mathcal{F}_0$-measurable, on a finite index set  

$\mathcal{M}$. We call $M$ the message index (or simply message) and $\mathcal{M}$ the message set of a $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-code. For any $q\in(1,\infty)$ denote with $R_0:=\frac{1}{t}\log_q|\mathcal{M}|$ the maximum $q$-ary code rate of $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-codes. If $M$ is uniformly distributed, then its entropy satisfies $\frac{1}{t}H(M)=R_0$. Traditionally the message $M$ is assumed to be uniformly distributed because the channel coding problem can be reduced to the case of a uniform message by a particular choice of random encoding [95] (p. 73). For biochemical systems, however, we acknowledge that the meaning of a message may be tied to its encoding, which precludes the traditional reduction method. Therefore, we allow for arbitrary message distributions and define the mean code rate [96]  

$$

R : = \frac { 1 } { t } H ( M ) \leq R _ { 0 } .

$$  

The symbol $R$, without any index, is reserved for code rates in this section. For notational simplicity we assume that all parallel reactions $r_1, r_2 \in \mathcal{R}$, $r_1 \neq r_2$, satisfy $\nu_{r_1} = \nu_{r_2}$, i.e., the change vectors have the same orientation for $\varepsilon = +$. For a CRN $U$ with $|\mathcal{R}|$ potentially reversible reaction channels we redefine $\mathcal{V}$ (cf. (11)) to contain only forward change vectors, i.e.,  

$$

\mathcal { V } : = \{ \mathbf { v } _ { r } \mid r \in \mathcal { R } \} \setminus \{ 0 \} \subset \mathbb { Z } ^ { | \mathcal { I } | } .

$$  

As before, assume $\mathcal{I}_X, \mathcal{I}_Y$ are disjoint subsets of species. The marginal reaction counters of $X$ are  

$$

R _ { s } ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) : = \sum _ { r \in V ^ { - 1 } ( \pi _ { X } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) ) } R _ { r } ^ { \varepsilon } ( s )

$$  

for all $\nu_X \in \mathcal{V}_X$ and $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$. Reaction counters for $Y$ are defined analogously. Consequently, we have  

$$

X ( s ) = X ( 0 ) + \sum _ { \mathbf { v } _ { X } \in \mathcal { V } _ { X } } \mathbf { v } _ { X } \left( R _ { s } ^ { X } ( + \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) - R _ { s } ^ { X } ( - \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) \right)

$$  

for all $s \in [0, t]$ and analogously for $Y$. At time zero, the system $U$ be always prepared with a well defined initial distribution $p(u, 0)$ for every independent use of the communication channel (and hence independent of $M$).  

Assigned to each message index $m \in \mathcal{M}$ is a deterministic manipulation protocol $\Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{X}}$ of the core CRN $U$ with bounded, piecewise-differentiable càdlàg trajectories on the interval $[0, t]$. This protocol is turned "on" at $t=0$. Importantly, $m \mapsto \Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{X}}$ be a bijective mapping. Next, we define the $\mathcal{F}_{0}$-measurable message process $\theta$, such that for all $s \in [0, t]$  

$$

\theta ( \omega , s ) : = \Gamma _ { M ( \omega ) } ^ { \mathrm { x } } ( s ) .

$$  

The manipulation protocols $\Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{X}}$ have the superscript X as the effect of the manipulation is assumed to solely affect certain reaction channels $r \in \mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}^{\circ}$ that exclusively change species X:  

$$

\mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } ^ { \circ } \subseteq \{ r | \pi _ { X } ( V ( r ) ) \neq 0 , \, \pi _ { X } ^ { \mathrm { c } } ( V ( r ) ) = 0 \} \subseteq \mathcal { R } ^ { \mathrm { X } } ,

$$  

where $\pi_{X}^{\mathcal{C}}: \mathcal{V} \rightarrow \mathbb{Z}^{|\mathcal{S}_{X}^{\mathcal{C}}|}$ is the coordinate map to coordinates of $\mathcal{S}_{X}^{\mathcal{C}} := \mathcal{S} \setminus \mathcal{S}_{X}$, complementary to $\mathcal{S}_{X}$.  

The propensity functions for reaction channels $r \in \mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}^{\circ}$ on $[0, t]$ depend on $m \in \mathcal{M}$ through the protocol $\Gamma_{m}^{\times}$, which we  

denote as $\lambda_{r}^{\varepsilon}(u,\Gamma_{m}^{\mathbf{x}}(s))$. If the protocols are non-constant, then the propensities are explicitly time-dependent, given $m$. All other reactions $r'\notin\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{\circ}$ are assumed to have no explicit time-dependence for simplicity.  

While $\theta$ can be regarded a (noiseless) source encoding, we refer to $X$ as the (noisy) channel encoding with channel encoding propensities  

$$

\lambda ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \nu _ { X } , u , \Gamma _ { m } ^ { \tt X } ( s ) ) : = \sum _ { r \in V ^ { - 1 } ( \pi _ { X } ^ { - 1 } ( \nu _ { X } ) ) } \lambda _ { r } ^ { \varepsilon } ( u , \Gamma _ { m } ^ { \tt X } ( s ) )

$$  

analogous to (15), where we do not distinguish between $m$-dependent and $m$-independent reactions on the r.h.s. for brevity. We call $Y$ the channel output with output propensities  

$$

\lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathfrak { v } _ { Y } , u ) : = \sum _ { r \in V ^ { - 1 } ( \pi _ { Y } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathfrak { v } _ { Y } ) ) } \lambda _ { r } ^ { \varepsilon } ( u ) .

$$  

Given the above specifications for the CRN, we introduce intensity processes that depend solely on the history of the general communication model $M$, $\theta$, $X$ and $Y$. The $\mathcal{F}^{\theta XY}$-intensities  

$$

\lambda _ { \mathrm { s } } ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) : = \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } , U ( s ^ { - } ) , \pmb { \theta } ( s ^ { - } ) ) \Big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { \theta X Y } \right] ,

$$  

are called feedback channel encoding intensities, and the $\mathcal{F}^{MXY}$-intensities  

$$

\lambda _ { s } ^ { M \rightarrow X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) : = \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } , U ( s ^ { - } ) , \theta ( s ^ { - } ) ) \big | \mathcal { F } _ { s - } ^ { M X Y } \right] ,

$$  

are called feedback source-channel encoding intensities. Further, the $\mathcal{F}^{XY}$-intensities  

$$

\lambda _ { s } ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } ) : = \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { X Y } \right] ,

$$  

are called (causal) channel output intensities. As we will discuss later, these channel output intensities represent the causal communication channel. For the evaluation of the relevant mutual information of a channel with feedback (and memory) yet another intensity is required. The $\mathcal{F}^{MY}$-intensities  

$$

\lambda _ { s } ^ { M \rightarrow Y } ( \varepsilon \mathcal { V } _ { Y } ) : = \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathcal { V } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \, | \, \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { M Y } \right] ,

$$  

we call source-output intensities.  

For the given setup with noiseless source encoding it can be shown that the source-channel encoding intensities are equal to the channel encoding intensities. For the formal statement of this equivalence we need the notion of the $\mathcal{F}$-predictable $\sigma$-algebra on $(0,\infty) \times \Omega$ [57](p. 8):  

$$

\mathcal { P } ( \mathcal { F } ) : = \sigma \left( ( s , \infty ) \times A \colon A \in \mathcal { F } _ { s } , s \geq 0 \right)

$$  

Then $((0,\infty)\times\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}),\mu\otimes\mathbb{P})$ is a measure space, where $\mu$ denotes the Lebesgue measure. For our purpose we only look at the restricted space $(0,t]\times\Omega$ and denote restricted filtrations as $\mathcal{F}_{[0,t]}$.  

Proposition 2. If $\theta$ is a noiseless source encoding, i.e., it is defined as specified above, then the channel encoding intensities are equivalent to the source-channel encoding intensities. That is, for all $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$ and $\mathbf{v}_X \in \mathcal{V}_X$ it holds that  

$$

\lambda ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathfrak { v } _ { X } ) \! = \! \lambda ^ { M \to X } ( \varepsilon \mathfrak { v } _ { X } ) \quad ( \mu \otimes \mathbb { P } ) \mathrm { - a . e . ~ o n ~ } \mathcal { P } ( \mathcal { F } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { X Y } \vee \sigma ( M ) ) .

$$  

Further, the source-output intensities satisfy  

$$

\lambda _ { s } ^ { M \rightarrow Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } ) = \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \Big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { \theta Y } \right] ,

$$  

$(\mu \otimes \mathbb{P})$-a.e. on $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,t]}^{Y} \vee \sigma(M))$, for all $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$ and $v_{Y} \in \mathcal{V}_{Y}$.  

A proof is presented in Supp. Sec. S1.6 A. Lastly, let  

$$

D \colon \mathcal { D } ( [ 0 , t ] , \mathcal { V } ) \to \mathcal { M } \cup \{ 0 \}

$$  

be a deterministic decoding function, which maps every output trajectory back to either of the messages in $\mathcal{M}$ or declares an erasure, by mapping to zero. We may associate two distinct conceptions with this decoding function: (i) the extrinsic decoder is a (computationally) learned function by an external observer, based on experimental observations of $Y_{[0,t]}$ given $M$; (ii) the intrinsic decoder refers to the conception that each $m \in \mathcal{M}$ stands for a pair of input and output of a bijective function, where $\Gamma_{m}^{\times}$ is the input and the output is a deterministic downstream action implemented by the biological cell. However, in the following we consider an abstract decoder which just determines the function space required for information theoretic analyses of chemical communication models. The (mean) probability of a decoding error be defined as  

$$

P _ { e } : = \mathbb { P } ( M \neq D ( Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) )

$$  

While other performance measures, like the maximum probability of error $\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}(M\neq D(Y_{[0,t]})\mid M=m)$ exist, we focus on the definition (56).  

In general, the relevant mutual information for a continuous-time chemical communication channel with memory and use of feedback is  

$$

\mathbb { I } ( M ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) \! = \! \sum _ { \varepsilon } \sum _ { v _ { Y } \in \mathcal { Y } _ { Y } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \mathbb { E } \left[ \phi ( \lambda _ { s } ^ { M \to Y } ( \varepsilon v _ { Y } ) ) - \phi ( \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon v _ { Y } ) ) \right] \mathrm { d } s ,

$$  

where we used that $\mathbb{I}(M;U(0))=0$ by construction. Consistently with Proposition 2 we have  

$$

\mathbb { I } ( M ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) = \mathbb { I } ( \theta _ { [ 0 , t ] } ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) = \mathbb { I } ( \theta _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } )

$$  

for a noiseless source-encoding $\theta_{[0,t]}$.  

Now let us elaborate on the construction of the communication system by deriving its (extended) local independence graph $\mathcal{G} = (\mathcal{I} \cup \mathcal{R}; \mathcal{B}, \mathcal{A})$. Here, $\mathcal{I} := \{\text{M}\} \cup \mathcal{I}$, where M is the label for the node of the baseline variable $M$, whose realization is equivalent to a full trajectory $\theta_{[0,t]}$. Mainly, $\mathcal{G}$ is derived from the SR-graph of $U$ as outlined in Sec. VC, augmented with the baseline vertex M. Hence, the least-assumptions edge sets are  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { A } = \bigcup _ { r \in \mathcal { R } } \left( \{ ( r ^ { \prime } , r ) \in \mathcal { R } ^ { 2 } \mid r ^ { \prime } \in \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( r ) ) \setminus \{ r \} \right\} } \\ & { \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad
