\qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \

$$  

and  

$$

\mathcal { B } = \left\{ \left\{ d , d ^ { \prime } \right\} \subset \mathcal { S } \mid d \neq d ^ { \prime } \right\} .

$$  

In the absence of further restrictions on the chemical communication system, choosing $(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{B})$ as a complete subgraph is consistent with the generic conditional independence structure induced by chemical reaction dynamics. A time-unrolled description of $U$ as a dynamic Bayesian network, with species population values $(\ldots,U(-2\mathrm{d}s),U(-\mathrm{d}s),U(0))$ at previous infinitesimal time layers, is sparsely connected across adjacent layers, but marginalization over the unobserved reaction history typically produces a fully connected conditional independence graph on $U(0)$ for any non-infinitesimal time horizon. Postulating a missing edge $\{d,d'\}\notin\mathcal{B}$ therefore amounts to requiring that $U_d(0)\perp U_{d'}(0)\mid U_{\mathcal{S}\setminus\{d,d'\}}(0)$ holds uniformly over the admissible choices of stoichiometry, reaction propensities, and preparation protocols for $p(u,0)$. A sparser $\mathcal{B}$ thus presupposes dynamically decoupled modules (e.g., bivariate subsystems) or a specific ergodic preparation protocol that asymptotically enforces such separations. To keep the framework applicable to general open CRNs with arbitrary preprocessing, we therefore adopt the structurally least restrictive choice and take $(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{B})$ to be complete.  

In a proper communication model, the message $M$ must be locally independent of $U$, which we have achieved through source-encoding via $\mathcal{F}_0$-measurable manipulation protocols. Note that $M$ and $U_{[0,t]}$ are not independent random variables since $M \rightarrow U$. Now assume that  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { R } \cap \mathrm { p a } ( \mathrm { c h } ( \mathsf { M } ) ) \subseteq \mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } ^ { \circ } , } \\ & { \mathcal { S } \cap \mathrm { p a } ( \mathrm { c h } ( \mathsf { M } ) ) \subseteq \mathcal { S } _ { X } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { Y } } \end{array}

$$  

i.e., all reaction-co-parents of M are in $\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{X}$ and all species-co-parents are in $\mathcal{I}_{X}\cup\mathcal{I}_{Y}$. Then, by construction and the global dynamic Markov property, we have that  

$$

\mathbf { M } \nrightarrow \tilde { \mathcal { R } } \mid _ { \mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } ^ { \circ } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { X } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { Y } } .

$$  

for all non-empty sets $\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{\prec}$. In particular, we then have  

$$

\mathbf { M } \nvdash \mathcal { R } ^ { Y } \mid \mathcal { R } ^ { X } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { X } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { Y } .

$$  

If additionally, there are no $X$- and no $Y$-indistinguishable re-actions, then Theorem 3 implies  

$$

\mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } | | X _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) = \mathbb { I } ( M \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } | | X _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) = 0 ,

$$  

where the first equality follows since source-encoding is noiseless and bijective. The property in (57) is actually an important classification of a communication system that was first introduced by Massey [11] for discrete-time models. We provide an adaptation of this definition to our model class.  

Definition 12. For a ($|\mathcal{M}|,t$)-source code with message $M$, let $\theta_{[0,t]}$ be a (potentially noisy) source encoding and $Y_{[0,t]}$ be the channel output. A chemical communication system $(M,\theta,U,\mathcal{S}_X,\mathcal{S}_Y)$ with designated channel encoding $\hat{X}_{[0,t]}$ is called causal if  

$$

( s , \varpi ) \mapsto \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathsf { v } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \Big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { X Y } \vee \mathcal { F } _ { t } ^ { \theta } \right]

$$  

is a $\mathcal{F}_{[0,t]}^{XY}$-intensity for all $\epsilon\in\{+,-\}$ and all $\nu_Y\in\mathcal{V}_Y$.  

A direct consequence is that causal communication systems satisfy  

$$

\mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } \left\| X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \right\} ) = 0 .

$$  

For a noiseless source-encoding Definition 12 is actually equivalent to $\mathbb{I}(M\rightarrow Y_{[0,t]}\|X_{[0,t]})=0$ by $\sigma(M)=\mathcal{F}_t^\theta$ and Theorem 3. Similar to Massey, we obtain the following inequality for any causal chemical communication system.  

Proposition 3. Let $(\theta_{[0,t]},X_{[0,t]},Y_{[0,t]})$ be a causal communication system for a $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-source code $\theta_{[0,t]}$. Then  

$$

\mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) \leq \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } )

$$  

with equality if and only if $\mathbb{I}(X_{[0,t]} \rightarrow Y_{[0,t]}||\theta_{[0,t]}) = 0.$  

Proof. Since $U(0)$ is prepared independently of $\theta(0)$ it holds that $\mathbb{I}(\theta(0);Y(0))=0$. Further, $\theta_{[0,t]}$ is locally independent from $Y_{[0,t]}$ by construction. Hence $\mathbb{I}(Y_{[0,t]}\rightarrow\theta_{[0,t]})=0$. Consequently,  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) = \mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) } \\ & { \leq \mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) + \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } | | \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) } \\ & { = \mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } , X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) } \\ & { = \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) + \mathbb { I } ( \pmb { \theta } _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } | | X _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) } \\ & { = \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) , } \end{array}

$$  

where we used (8) in the first line, non-negativity of the causally conditioned DI in the second line, the chain rule for DI in the third line and (58) in the last line. $\square$  

In that, $\mathbb{I}(X_{[0,t]} \rightarrow Y_{[0,t]}||\theta_{[0,t]})$ quantifies the surplus of causal information provided by $X$ to $Y$ without the causal information already provided by $\theta$. We can think of this as the amount of channel encoding noise that is causally transmitted to $Y$. Since the $\mathcal{F}^{\theta Y}$-intensities  

$$

\mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \Big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { \theta Y } \right]

$$  

usually differ from the $\mathcal{F}^{\theta XY}$-intensities  

$$

\mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \Big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { \theta X Y } \right]

$$  

for any $\nu_{Y} \in \mathcal{V}_{Y}$, $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$, this surplus information typically has a positive value. Such positive surplus information can be exemplarily verified with the small models in Sec. VIC under the assumption of just two different constant chemostat concentration values as $\Gamma_{m}^{\times}$-trajectories.  

# 2. The noisy channel coding problem in chemical communication  

In the standard noisy channel coding problem for a causal communication system, the causally conditioned channel law $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{y\|X}$ is taken as fixed (see Appendix C for a definition). Here, we discuss the notion of channel capacity in the context of chemical communication.  

The channel capacity is a property of a causal chemical channel ($\mathcal{S}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X}, \mathcal{S}_Y$) with fixed input and output species identities and a fixed channel law. Communicators may choose a code design – an encoder (the dynamics of $X$) and a decoder (post-processing of $Y$) – but they cannot alter the physical medium or its intrinsic randomness. Accordingly, the fluctuations that drive $Y$ beyond those induced by $X$ are modeled as the channel's internal noise, which is not under the communicators' control. This setup cleanly separates "code design" from "channel control". Allowing $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\parallel X}$ to change turns the problem into a joint code–channel co-design task. While such co-design may be a reasonable modeling choice for biochemical systems in some cases, we deliberately focus on the traditional fixed-channel formulation within our chemical communication model.  

The channel law $\mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X}$ is equivalently represented by the collection of predictable intensities $\{\lambda^{Y}(\varepsilon v_{Y})\mid\varepsilon\in\{+,-\},v_{Y}\in\mathcal{V}_{Y}\}$, which is unique up to stochastic equivalence [57] (p. 31). This equivalence is readily apparent from the path likelihood (23). Whereas much of the literature formulates such likelihoods on the marked-jump space $\bigcup_{n=0}^{n}\left([0,t]\times(\mathcal{V}_{Y}\cup-\mathcal{V}_{Y})\right)^{n}$, we work with an equivalent, and more intuitive, representation on the space of càdlàg trajectories $\mathscr{D}([0,t],\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y})$. To this end, we introduce a collection of predictable non-negative functionals  

chosen such that  

on the predictable space $\left([0, t] \times \Omega, \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}_{[0, t]}^{XY})\right)$. Their existence is guaranteed by the factorization lemma [97], and they can be chosen as the regenerative version specified by Brémaud [57] (p. 63).  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon v _ { Y } ) = \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { Y } } ^ { Y } ( \cdot , ( X , Y ) _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) , \qquad ( \mu \otimes \mathbb { P } ) \mathrm { - a . e . } } \end{array}

$$  

With this notation, the causally conditioned path likelihood admits the form  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { L _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { Y | X } ( x _ { [ 0 , t ] } , y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) } \\ & { = \! \exp \left( - \sum _ { \varepsilon } \sum _ { v _ { Y } \in \mathcal { V } _ { Y } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { Y } } ^ { Y } ( s , ( x , y ) _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) \, \mathrm { d } s \right) } \\ & { \quad \times \exp \left( \sum _ { \varepsilon } \sum _ { v _ { Y } \in \mathcal { V } _ { Y } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \ln \big ( \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { Y } } ^ { Y } ( s , ( x , y ) _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) \big ) \, \mathrm { d } \rho _ { s } ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon v _ { Y } ) \right) } \end{array}

$$  

where the counting (process) trajectories $\rho^{Y}(\varepsilon\nu_{Y})$ are fully determined by $y_{[0,t]}$.  

For coding with transmission duration $t$ and message $M$, and under the constraint that the channel law $\mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X}$ is kept invariant, the remaining degrees of freedom are: (i) the $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$ source code, which specifies the functional form of the protocols $\Gamma_{m}^{\mathbb{X}}$ on $[0,t]$; (ii) the feedback channel encoding, i.e., the causal law $\mathbb{P}^{X\parallel\theta,Y}$; and (iii) the initial distribution $p(u,0)$, if it can be manipulated.  

As in the output case, the feedback encoding law $\mathbb{P}^{X\parallel\theta,Y}$ can be represented by the collection of predictable input intensi  

ties {$\lambda^{X}(\varepsilon v_{X}) \mid \varepsilon \in \{+, -\}, v_{X} \in \mathcal{V}_{X}$} (unique up to stochastic equivalence). In particular, we may choose predictable functionals  

$$

\left\{ \Lambda _ { \pmb { \varepsilon } , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ^ { X } \mid \pmb { \varepsilon } \in \{ + , - \} , \mathbf { v } _ { X } \in \mathcal { V } _ { X } \right\}

$$  

such that, on the predictable space $([0,t]\times\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,t]}^{\theta XY}))$,  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \lambda ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) = \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ^ { X } \big ( \cdot , ( \theta , X , Y ) _ { [ 0 , t ] } \big ) , \qquad ( \mu \otimes \mathbb { P } ) \mathrm { - a . e . } } \end{array}

$$  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \left\{ \Lambda _ { \pmb { \varepsilon } , \mathbf { v } _ { Y } } ^ { Y } : [ 0 , t ] \times \mathcal { D } ( [ 0 , t ] , \mathcal { X } \times \mathcal { Y } ) \to \mathbb { R } _ { \geq 0 } \, | \, \pmb { \varepsilon } \in \{ + , - \} , \mathbf { v } _ { Y } \in \mathcal { V } _ { Y } \right\} } \end{array}

$$  

Equivalently, for any fixed protocol $\Gamma_{m}^{\mathbb{X}}$ and history $(x,y)_{[0,t]}$, the map $s\mapsto\Lambda_{\varepsilon,\nu_{\mathbb{X}}}^{X}(s,\Gamma_{m}^{\mathbb{X}},(x,y)_{[0,t]})$ specifies a version of the input intensity along that trajectory. Accordingly, the design space of admissible channel codes can be identified with the class of such predictable functionals (subject to regularity constraints). Replacing this family by an alternative collection  

$$

\{ \breve { \Lambda } _ { \pmb { \varepsilon } , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ^ { X } \mid \pmb { \varepsilon } \in \{ + , - \} , \, \mathbf { v } _ { X } \in \breve { \mathcal { V } } _ { X } \}

$$  

induces, in general, a different joint measure $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$ on $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}^{M\theta XY})$. The invariance constraint on the physical channel is precisely that the conditional output law remains unchanged, i.e., "$\mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X} = \tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{Y\parallel X}$". A class of such admissible channel codes will be derived in Sec. VI A 5, Theorem 5.  

Finally, degrees (i) and (ii) may be combined into a single causal feedback source-channel encoding law $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel M,Y}$ on $[0,t]$, which is equivalently represented by predictable intensities for the message-driven input process. Concretely, one may introduce predictable functionals  

$$

\{ \Lambda _ { \pmb { \varepsilon } , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ^ { M \rightarrow X } \mid \pmb { \varepsilon } \in \{ + , - \} , \, \mathbf { v } _ { X } \in \mathcal { V } _ { X } \}

$$  

such that  

$$

\lambda _ { \mathrm { s } } ^ { M \to X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) = \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ^ { M \to X } \big ( s , M , ( X , Y ) _ { [ 0 , t ] } \big ) \, , \qquad ( \mu \otimes \mathbb { P } ) \mathrm { - a . e . }

$$  

A more formal notion of codes is presented in Appendix E. Next we turn to the definition of a suitable class of code families, which is required for the formulation of asymptotic limiting properties.  

In classical channel coding, longer block codes are often built by concatenating codewords in time. Given a $(|\mathcal{M}_a|,t_a)$-code and a $(|\mathcal{M}_b|,t_b)$-code, one sends a message $(m_a,m_b)\in\mathcal{M}_a\times\mathcal{M}_b$ by sending a $(|\mathcal{M}_a|,t_a)$-codeword and, after a channel and feedback reset (or under a memoryless channel), sending a $(|\mathcal{M}_b|,t_b)$-codeword. Such a code scheme enforces that single codewords are sent via independent uses of the channel. While this works well for memoryless channels, it may result in practically low code rates for channels with memory as the guard/relaxation intervals for resets to the a prescribed channel initialization $p_0$ can be substantial and may dominate the communication time [98]. Previous continuous-time capacity results have typically assumed a memoryless channel [24, 99] and feedback with instantaneous resets [12]. For channels with memory, where small guard times lead to intersymbol interference across code blocks [98], it is therefore favorable, both theoretically and practically, to use a single code with a larger codebook, spanning the whole transmission, instead of  

a sequence of codewords that are decoded independently under intersymbol interference. Continuous-time collections of messages and codes of practical and theoretical interest will hence be in the following class.  

Definition 13. A collection $(M_t)_{t\geq0}$ of messages $M_t\colon\Omega\to\mathcal{M}_t$ is called monotonous if $|\mathcal{M}_{t'}|\geq|\mathcal{M}_t|$ for all $t'>t>0$. Any collection of codes $(C_t)_{t\geq0}$ for $(M_t)_{t\geq0}$ is also called monotonous.  

While this definition does not ensure that the code rate $t \mapsto R(t) = \frac{1}{t}H(M_t)$ is a monotonously increasing function, it formally accounts for the time-concatenation idea in continuous-time for a single channel use and prevents the maximum code rate $R_0(t)$ to vanish at finite times. For instance, the time-concatenation of potentially interference-aware $(|\mathcal{M}_0|, \Delta t)$ code-slots with $|\mathcal{M}_0| \geq 2$ results in a time-dependent maximum code rate  

$$

t \mapsto R _ { 0 } ( t ) = \frac { \log ( | \mathcal { M } _ { 0 } | ) } { t } \left| \frac { t } { \Delta t } \right| \leq \frac { \log ( | \mathcal { M } _ { 0 } | ) } { \Delta t } = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \to \infty } R _ { 0 } ( t ) .

$$  

Since, unlike in discrete-time lossless coding (where the per-symbol rate is bounded), a continuum-valued protocol can in principle encode countably many messages on an arbitrarily short interval [0,$\Delta t$), one could make the apparent code rate arbitrarily large by choosing $\Delta t$ very small.  

Having introduced practical issues of code design for chemical communication, we now turn to the notions of achievability and capacity required for coding theorems. The following definitions are adapted from Csiszár and Körner [100].  

Definition 14. Let $\varepsilon \in [0,1)$ and the code $\mathcal{C}$ be consistent with $\mathbb{P}$. The code $\mathcal{C}$ is called an $(|\mathcal{M}|,t,\varepsilon)$-code for the message $M$ if  

$$

P _ { \epsilon } = \mathbb { P } \left( M \neq D ( Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) \right) \leq \epsilon .

$$  

Definition 15 ($\varepsilon$-achievable rates and $\varepsilon$-capacity). For $\varepsilon \in [0,1)$, a nonnegative number $R^*$ is an $\varepsilon$-achievable rate for the channel $(\mathcal{S}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\|X}, \mathcal{S}_Y)$ if for all $\delta > 0$ exists $t_0 > 0$ such that for all $t \geq t_0$ exists a message $M_t : \Omega \to \mathcal{M}_t$ such that  

(i) $R^* - \delta \leq \frac{1}{l}H(M_t)$ and  

(ii) exists an $(|\mathcal{M}_t|,t,\mathcal{E})$-code for $M_t$.  

A number $R^*$ is an achievable rate if it is $\varepsilon$-achievable for all $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$.  

The supremum of $\varepsilon$-achievable rates, respectively, achievable rates is called $\varepsilon$-capacity $C_{\varepsilon}$, respectively, capacity $C$ of the channel $(\mathcal{S}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\|X}, \mathcal{S}_Y)$.  

Since $C_{\varepsilon}$ decreases as $\varepsilon \searrow 0$, we have  

$$

C = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { \varepsilon \in ( 0 , 1 ) } C _ { \varepsilon } = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { \varepsilon \searrow 0 } C _ { \varepsilon } .

$$  

In line with the existing literature, we explicitly point out that the collection of messages $(M_t)_{t\geq0}$ is subject to design and is not fixed in the noisy channel coding problem. However, as we pointed out when defining code rates, semantic aspects in  

biochemical systems may constrain admissible message random variables (and their distributions) in practice.  

The information capacity $C_{\mathbb{I}}$ for any information stable channel $(\mathcal{S}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\|X}, \mathcal{S}_Y)$ be defined as  

$$

C _ { \mathbb { I } } : = \operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { t \to \infty } C _ { \mathbb { I } } ( t ) \quad \mathrm { w i t h }

$$  

$$

c _ { \mathbb { I } } ( t ) : = \underset { M _ { t } } { \operatorname* { s u p } } \, \underset { \theta _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \operatorname* { s u p } } \, \underset { X _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \operatorname* { s u p } } \, \frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { I } ( M _ { t } ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } )

$$  

where $\sup_{X_{[0,t]}}$ optimizes over the families of functions $\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon,v_X}^X \mid \varepsilon \in \{+,-\}, v_X \in \mathcal{V}_X\}$, and thereby implicitly over the feedback channel encoding distribution $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel\theta,Y}$, as well as the initial distribution $p(u,0)$ under the constraint that it leaves the channel law invariant. Further, $\sup_{\theta_{[0,t]}}$ optimizes over the space of source encoding protocol functions $\Gamma_m^X$ and $\sup_{M_t}$ optimizes over the cardinality of $\mathcal{M}_t$ and well as the distribution of $M_t$.  

The limit in (59) is essential for defining information capacity because, in principle, it enables identification with the operational channel capacity, i.e., the supremum of rates $R$ for which there exists a sequence of codes with $P_e \to 0$ as $t \to \infty$. Establishing this equivalence lies beyond the scope of the present work and we confine ourselves to the converse channel coding theorem (cf. Theorem 4). In classical definitions of the channel capacity for discrete-time systems with memory and feedback, the latter two suprema are typically not involved in the definition. Optimizing over $\theta_{[0,t]}$ and $M_t$ can actually be subsumed into the optimization over $X_{[0,t]}$ for causal systems both channel encoding and source encoding are noiseless (i.e., one-to-one mappings). Since the channel encoding is always noisy for chemical communication, only $\sup_{M_t}$ can be subsumed into $\sup_{\theta_{[0,t]}}$ for noiseless source coding. Similarly, if a (joint) source-channel code is used instead of a split source-channel code, then the remaining optimization problems are $\sup_{M_t} \sup_{X_{[0,t]}}$ since $\theta$ is not explicitly modeled.  

For intuition, we propose a generalization of information stability (as defined in [101]) to continuous-time channels, but we leave proving its applicability to future work.  

Definition 16. The channel $(\mathcal{S}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\|X}, \mathcal{S}_Y)$ is called information stable if there exists a sequence $\left((M_t^{(n)}, \theta_{[0,t]}^{(n)})_{t\geq0}, X^{(n)}\right)$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$ such that for all $\delta>0$  

$$

\operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { n \to \infty } \mathbb { P } ^ { ( n ) } \left( \left| \frac { 1 } { n } \ln \frac { \mathrm { d } \mathbb { P } ^ { Y | M _ { t } ^ { ( n ) } } } { \mathrm { d } \mathbb { P } ^ { Y ( n ) } } - C _ { \mathbb { I } } \right| > \delta \right) = 0

$$  

(i.e., with convergence in probability).  

Here, we added the sequence of processes/distributions since the capacity is typically not achieved by any element of the optimization space. Information stability is typically satisfied for ergodic communication system processes.  

What is not explicit in (60) is that the function spaces over which we optimize are typically restricted by additional con  

straints, such as upper bounds on reaction rates, a total maximum energy budget and confinements on the practical degrees of freedom in $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel\theta,Y}$ for chemical systems. We make energetic constraints explicit in the section VI A 3 and give a converse coding theorem, while practical degrees of freedom are discussed in VI A 5.  

# 3. Channel coding under energetic constraints  

Let us now introduce a physically principled constraint on the search space of $X_{[0,t]}$ and $\theta_{[0,t]}$. For each $m \in \mathcal{M}_t$ we consider the heat $q_m(t)$ dissipated by the trajectory $U_{[0,t]}$. Let $Q_0 \geq 0$ be the upper bound on the time-averaged heat, such that  

$$

\frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { E } [ q _ { m } ( t ) ] \leq Q _ { 0 } .

$$  

Such a constraint is also called input-output cost function [95], because it considers the dissipative cost of the whole CRN $U$ and not only the energy dissipated by channel encoding into $X$. Evaluating the finite-time information capacity under this constraint yields the thermodynamic capacity-cost functions  

$$

Q _ { 0 } \mapsto C _ { \mathbb { I } } ( Q _ { 0 } ) , \quad Q _ { 0 } \mapsto c _ { \mathbb { I } } ( t , Q _ { 0 } ) .

$$  

Alternatively, we can also constrain the chemical work or the total entropy production  

$$

\frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { E } [ w ^ { \mathrm { c } } ( t ) ] \leq W _ { 0 } , \quad \frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { E } [ h _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { l o t } } ( t ) ] \leq \varepsilon _ { 0 }

$$  

with $W_0, \varepsilon_0 \geq 0$, leading to other thermodynamic capacity-cost functions. If the protocols $\Gamma_m^{\mathbb{X}}$ are constant protocols, where $m$ just specifies a different concentration of chemostat species, then both the heat dissipation and the entropy production constraint can be rewritten in the limit $t \to \infty$, such that  

$$

e _ { p } \leq \beta Q _ { 0 } \quad \mathrm { o r } \quad e _ { p } \leq \varepsilon _ { 0 }

$$  

by virtue of (52).  

Further alternatives, that aim to (i) approximate the cost, if no information about the internal entropy $h_{\mathrm{i}}$ is available, or (ii) isolate the thermodynamic cost of the channel encoding reactions (excluding the cost of "pure" transmission) may use the above constraints, but with (46), (39) and (49). In the case of pure encoding cost, we would just sum over reaction channels $\mathcal{R}_{r}$ that are directly coupled to the protocols $\Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{X}}$.  

For the given communication channel we can also provide a converse (channel coding) theorem [20, 24], which is valid for any of the discussed energy constraints. Importantly, it is also valid for information unstable channels, since it only provides an upper bound on the operational capacity that is not necessarily tight.  

Lemma 2. Consider an $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-code for the channel $(\mathcal{S}_X,\mathbb{P}^Y\|_X,\mathcal{S}_Y)$ with message $M:\Omega\to\mathcal{M}$. If the code exhibits the code rate $R$, then  

$$

R \leq \frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { I } ( M ; Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) + \frac { 1 } { t } H _ { B } \left( P _ { e } \right) + P _ { e } R _ { 0 } ,

$$  

where $H_{B}(p) = -p\log(p) - (1-p)\log(1-p)$ denotes the binary entropy function (for an arbitrary log-base).  

Theorem 4. Let R be an achievable rate of the channel ($\mathcal{I}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X}, \mathcal{I}_Y$) under a given set of energy constraints on admissible codes. For simplicity, we exemplarily use the heat dissipation constraint with upper bound $Q_0$. Then it holds  

$$

R \leq C ( Q _ { 0 } ) \leq C _ { \mathbb { I } } ( Q _ { 0 } ) ,

$$  

where $C(Q_0)$ denotes the operational capacity under the dissipation constraint.  

Proofs for both, the Lemma and the Theorem, is provided in Supp. Sec. S1.6 A.  

Lastly, although we formulate these optimization problems under thermodynamic constraints, they should often be complemented by kinetic constraints. For instance, at thermodynamic equilibrium, where the rate constants satisfy (27), one could in principle let all rate constants diverge while maintaining fixed propensity ratios. This, however, is clearly unphysical. Additional assumptions, such as lower bounds on mean sojourn times [37] or explicit models of the reaction constants [64], are thus necessary to ensure physical plausibility. In stochastic thermodynamics, the interplay between thermodynamic and kinetic constraints has been explored in the context of thermodynamic and kinetic uncertainty relations [102].  

# 4. Energy-per-bit perspective  

A dual perspective to information capacity is the energy-per-bit-rate function [95]. Define the average energy per information unit (given the code rate $R$ in an arbitrary log-base) as  

$$

E _ { \mathrm { b } } ( R ) : = \frac { Q _ { 0 } } { R } .

$$  

Instead of the heat dissipation bound $Q_0$, we can of course also use any other bound. Since the capacity-cost functions $Q_0 \mapsto C_{\mathbb{I}}(Q_0)$ and $Q_0 \mapsto c_{\mathbb{I}}(t, Q_0)$ are monotonously increasing, the minimum-energy-per-bit-rate function can be defined as their generalized inverse:  

$$

E _ { \mathrm { b } } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ( R ) : = \operatorname* { i n f } \left\{ E _ { \mathrm { b } } \geq 0 | C _ { \mathbb { I } } ( E _ { \mathrm { b } } R ) \geq R \right\} ,

$$  

$$

e _ { \mathrm { b } } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ( t , R ) : = \operatorname* { i n f } \left\{ E _ { \mathrm { b } } \geq 0 | c _ { \mathbb { I } } ( t , E _ { \mathrm { b } } R ) \geq R \right\} .

$$  

Given Theorem 4, $E_{\mathrm{b}}^{\min}(R)$ is actually a lower bound on the minimum amount of heat that must be dissipated per unit of information sent to reliably communicate at code rate $R$ over a given channel. This bound is conjectured to be tight for information stable channels. The following Lemma makes the asymptotic version numerically more accessible.  

# Lemma 3. It holds that  

$$

E _ { \mathrm { b } } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( R ) = \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { t \to \infty } e _ { b } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( t , R ) .

$$  

A proof is provided in Supp. Sec. S1.6B.  

Similarly, we can define the minimum average heat dissipation required for a minimum information rate $R$:  

$$

q _ { \mathbb { I } } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ( t , R ) : = \underset { \theta _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \operatorname* { i n f } } \underset { X _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \operatorname* { i n f } } \frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { E } [ q ( s ) ] ,

$$  

subject to the constraint $\mathbb{I}(\theta_{[0,t]};Y_{[0,t]})\geq Rt$ for both infima. It's asymptotic version is  

$$

Q _ { \mathbb { I } } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ( R ) : = \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { t \to \infty } q _ { \mathbb { I } } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ( t , R ) .

$$  

The two presented notions of minimum energy dissipation per information unit are shown to be equivalent.  

Proposition 4.  

$$

e _ { \mathrm { b } } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( t , R ) \! = \! \frac { 1 } { R } q _ { \mathbb { I I } } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( t , R )

$$  

$$

E _ { \mathrm { b } } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( R ) \! = \! \frac { 1 } { R } Q _ { \mathbb { I } } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( R )

$$  

As before, the definitions and equivalence results work analogously with total entropy production, etc. A proof is provided in Supp. Sec. S1.6B.  

# 5. Degrees of freedom in channel coding  

Chemical reactions cannot be changed arbitrarily in reality. To find the supremum $\sup_{X_{[0,t]}}$ in the capacity definition, we have just a few options. First, we may apply "thermodynamic parameter tuning" by exchanging certain species with stoichiometrically equivalent ones that nonetheless exhibit different reaction affinities. That is, we change $g$, such that $\Delta_r^+ g$ is modified for a subset of reaction channel. Otherwise, we can change the fixed chemostat concentrations $z$ (not the source encoding protocols $\Gamma_m^X$), such that only those reaction channels are changed, that leave the channel probabilities invariant. Lastly, we can vary the reaction network topology by introducing or erasing reactions, i.e., adding and removing new stoichiometric vectors (including their chemostat couplings). This may also include a change in the number and identities of species that are not in $\mathcal{S}_X \cup \mathcal{S}_Y$. Again, all these modification must not change the channel $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\parallel X}$. This leaves us with the question of which reaction channels are actually allowed to be modified?  

We address this problem with a network-topological classification based on the species-reaction graph.  

Definition 17. Consider the reversible species-reaction graph of the open CRN U. A reaction channel $\mathcal{R}_r$ is classified as ...  

(a) feedforward ($\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{ff}}$) if there is a 'trail' from $\mathcal{S}_X$ to $\mathcal{S}_Y$ that passes through node $\mathcal{R}_r$.  

(b) feedback ($\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{fb}}$) if there is a 'trail' from $\mathcal{S}_{Y}$ to $\mathcal{S}_{X}$ that passes through node $\mathcal{R}_{r}$.  

(c) modulating ($\mathcal{R}_{c}$) if either of the following is true:  

(i) $\mathfrak{R}_{r}$ is purely modulating ($\mathcal{R}_{c}^{\circ}$) if it is neither a feed-forward nor a feedback reaction and there exists a 'trail' from node $\mathfrak{R}_{r}$ to $\mathcal{S}_{X} \cup \mathcal{S}_{Y}$.  

(ii) $\mathcal{R}_r$ is feedforward, but not feedback, and there exists a 'trail' from $\mathcal{R}_r$ to $\mathcal{S}_X$ that avoids $\mathcal{S}_Y$.  

(iii) $\mathcal{R}_r$ is feedback, but not feedforward, and there exists a 'trail' from $\mathcal{R}_r$ to $\mathcal{S}_Y$ that avoids $\mathcal{S}_X$.  

(d) disconnected ($\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{d}}$) if there is no 'trail' from node $\mathcal{R}_{r}$ to $\mathcal{S}_{X} \cup \mathcal{S}_{Y}$.  

(e) input modulating ($\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Tx}}$) if it is modulating and exists a 'trail' from $\mathcal{R}_{r}$ to $\mathcal{S}_{X}$ that avoids $\mathcal{S}_{Y}$.  

(f) output modulating ($\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{RX}}$) if it is modulating and exists a 'trail' from $\mathcal{R}_{r}$ to $\mathcal{S}_{Y}$ that avoids $\mathcal{S}_{X}$.  

(g) purely input modulating if $r \in \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Tx}}^{\circ} := \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\circ} \cap \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Tx}} \setminus \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Rx}}$.  

(h) purely output modulating if $r \in \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Rx}}^{\circ} := \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\circ} \cap \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Rx}} \setminus \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Tx}}$.  

The given classification rules are designed to emphasize two relevant functionalities that a reaction channel can exhibit. First, the directional information exchange between $X$ and $Y$ (feedforward and feedback), and second, modulation of either $X$ or $Y$ that does not exploit any information about the other. Disconnected reaction channels do not influence $X$ and $Y$ at all and are hence irrelevant to the communication system. The subclassifications within $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{c}}$ serve the practical interpretability. In Appendix D we additionally dissect the above rules in terms of binary "adjacency" matrices that indicate whether directed trails between $\mathcal{R}_{r}$ and either $\mathcal{S}_{X}$ or $\mathcal{S}_{Y}$ exist.  

We are led by the following intuition: reaction channels for which no trail from $\mathcal{R}_r$ to $\mathcal{I}_Y$ exists that avoids $\mathcal{I}_X$ should be modifiable without changing the channel $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\parallel X}$. Only trails that carry influence from a reaction channel to $Y$ without passing through $X$ can alter how inputs are mapped to outputs. Using the matrix representations in Appendix D, one verifies that the following set collects exactly these reactions, and its decomposition makes the functional roles transparent.  

Definition 18. Consider the reversible species-reaction graph of the open CRN U. Reaction channels in the set  

$$

\mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } : = \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { d } } \cup [ \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f b } } \setminus ( \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f f } } \cup \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { R x } } ) ] \cup \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { T x } } ^ { \circ }

$$  

are called encoding channels.  

Thus far, we have taken a reaction-centered perspective, but the species-centered perspective is always implicit. For example, changing a chemostat concentration, changes the propensities of a set of reactions, and erasing a core species for the network corresponds to erasing all reactions in which it participates.  

A degree of freedom in the optimization of $\sup_{X_{[0,t]}}$ that we did not address so far, is the initial distribution $p(u,0)$. At least for finite transmission duration $t$ we can expect that a  

well-chosen initial distribution can reduce the decoding error $P_e$ for a given source encoding $\theta_{[0,t]}$. The initial values of certain species have an effect only on $X_{[0,t]}$, while others also have a direct effect on $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\|X}$, e.g., because they are part of the species that implement feedforward or output modulation. Species that are directly connected to $\mathscr{I}_Y$ in the SR-graph can be identified with $\text{pa}_0(\mathscr{R}_{\leftarrow}^C)$, where we introduce “$(\ldots)^C$” (in contrast to “$(\ldots)^c$”) as the notation for the complement on either of the sets $\mathscr{R}$ or $\mathscr{I}$. That is, $\mathscr{R}_{\leftarrow}^C = \mathscr{R} \setminus \mathscr{R}_{\leftarrow}$ and $\left[\text{pa}_0(\mathscr{R}_{\leftarrow}^C)\right]^C = \mathscr{I} \setminus \text{pa}_0(\mathscr{R}_{\leftarrow}^C)$ are the species that do not have a direct connection to $\mathscr{I}_Y$. Since the Janossy density of $(X,Y)_{[0,t]}$, represented as a function of trajectories, admits the factorization  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { P } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { X , Y } \big ( x _ { [ 0 , t ] } , y _ { [ 0 , t ] } \big ) = \mathbb { P } \left( X ( 0 ) = x ( 0 ) , Y ( 0 ) = y ( 0 ) \right) } \\ & { \qquad \qquad \times \mathcal { P } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { Y \| X } \big ( x _ { [ 0 , t ] } , y _ { [ 0 , t ] } \big ) \ \mathcal { P } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { X \| Y } \big ( x _ { [ 0 , t ] } , y _ { [ 0 , t ] } \big ) , } \end{array}

$$  

the channel law is independent of the choice of initial distribution of the species $\mathcal{I}_X \cup \mathcal{I}_Y$.  

Definition 19. Consider the reversible species-reaction graph of the open CRN U. The species in the set  

$$

\mathcal { I } _ { \leftarrow } : = \left[ \mathrm { p a } _ { 0 } ( \mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } ^ { \mathbf { C } } ) \right] ^ { C } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { X } \cup \mathcal { S } _ { Y } ,

$$  

are called precoding (or, reset-encoding) species, where the complements are taken on $\mathcal{R}$ and $\mathcal{S}$, respectively.  

The following theorem asserts, that the intuition about $\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}$ and $\mathcal{I}_{\leftarrow}$ is correct for a particular class of systems.  

Let $(M,\theta,U,\mathcal{I}_X,\mathcal{I}_Y)$ describe a causal chemical communication system, as just outlined, but with the extension that manipulation protocols $\Gamma^X_m$ may affect not only $\mathcal{R}^X$, but all of $\mathcal{R}_\leftarrow$. Without restriction of generality, we assume that all propensity functions $\lambda^{\varepsilon}_r(u,m,t)$ have a non-empty support, i.e., they must admit positive values for some arguments. For all $r\in\mathcal{R}_\leftarrow$ let $\eta^{\varepsilon}_r(u,m,t)$ be alternative (reversible) reaction propensities, which may differ from the original $\lambda^{\varepsilon}_r(u,m,t)$ such that the stoichiometry of the reaction is unmodified. Either the reaction intensity is set to zero, i.e., $\eta^{\varepsilon}_r=0$ for all $\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}$ for all arguments, which corresponds to an erasure of the reaction from the network. Else, the reaction remains active, i.e., $\eta^{\varepsilon}_r(u,m,t)>0$ for some arguments. Then it needs to satisfy two properties:  

(i) Reaction stoichiometry of $\Re_{r}$ is unchanged, i.e., for all $u \neq u'$ with $(u_{d} : \pi_{d}(\nu_{r}) \neq 0) = (u'_{d} : \pi_{d}(\nu_{r}) \neq 0)$  

$$

\eta _ { r } ^ { \varepsilon } ( u , m , t ) = \eta _ { r } ^ { \varepsilon } ( u ^ { \prime } , m , t ) .

$$  

(ii) If $(u,m,t)$ satisfies $\eta_r^\varepsilon(u,m,t)>0$, then $\lambda_r^\varepsilon(u,m,t)>0$. This particularly restricts possible variations of $t$-dependence, given $m$.  

All other reactions keep their original propensities. Now denote $S_p := (U_d(0): d \in \mathcal{S}_{\leftarrow})$ and $S_c := (U_d(0): d \in \mathcal{S} \setminus$  

$\mathcal{S}_{\leftarrow}$), such that $U(0)=(S_{p},S_{c})$. The set of alternative propensities, together with a yet to be defined alternative initial distribution and an alternative message distribution, defines an alternative process on the probability space $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})$, such that the alternative path measure is given by $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{[0,t]}^{U}$. We stipulate that $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\circ S_{c}^{-1}=\mathbb{P}\circ S_{c}^{-1}$ and allow variations of $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{p}=\cdot\mid S_{c}=s_{c})$ for any $s_{c}$, as long as any $s_{p}$ with $\mathbb{P}(S_{p}=s_{p}\mid S_{c}=s_{c})>0$ also satisfies $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{p}=s_{p}\mid S_{c}=s_{c})>0$.  

Our specifications of the alternative propensities and allowed variations of the initial distribution ensure that the alternative path measure is absolutely continuous with respect to the original path measure. With the alternative probability measure, we define channel output intensities  

$$

\eta _ { s } ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathsf { v } _ { Y } ) : = \check { \mathbb { E } } \left[ \lambda ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathsf { v } _ { Y } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { X Y } \right] .

$$  

for all $s \in [0, t]$ and all $\nu_Y \in \mathcal{V}_Y$, $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$.  

Theorem 5. Consider two CRNs that differ only in reaction propensities of encoding reactions $\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}$ and the conditional initial distribution of precoding species $\mathcal{I}_{\leftarrow}$, as described above. If $\mathcal{I}_{\leftarrow}^{\mathrm{C}}=\emptyset$, $\operatorname{pa}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}\right) \cap \mathcal{R} \subseteq \mathcal{R}^{Y} \cup \mathcal{R}^{X}$ and there are no X- or Y-indistinguishable reactions, then  

$$

\lambda _ { s } ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } ) = \eta _ { s } ^ { Y } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { Y } ) , \quad \check { \mathbb { P } } \mathrm { - } a . s .

$$  

for all $\nu_Y \in \mathcal{V}_Y$, $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$ and all $s \in [0, t]$. In particular, the causally conditional path measures satisfy  

$$

\check { \mathbb { P } } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { Y \| X } = \mathbb { P } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { Y \| X } , \quad \check { \mathbb { P } } { - a . s . }

$$  

A proof is provided in Supp. Sec. S1.6 C.  

The equalities in Theorem 5 are stated $\check{\mathbb{P}}$-a.s. To assert them also $\check{\mathbb{P}}$-a.s., an additional dominance condition is required: for every $r \in \mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}$ and $\varepsilon \in \{+, -\}$, the alternative propensity $\eta_{r}^{\varepsilon}$ must not vanish on the support of the corresponding original propensity $\lambda_{r}^{\varepsilon}$. Equivalently, this ensures $\mathbb{P} \ll \check{\mathbb{P}}$ (in addition to the already guaranteed $\check{\mathbb{P}} \ll \mathbb{P}$), i.e., mutual absolute continuity. This subtlety has little practical impact. One may view $(\theta, U, \mathbb{P})$ as a "super-network" that contains at least all reactions and species present under $(\theta, U, \check{\mathbb{P}})$. Then every $\check{\mathbb{P}}$-possible path is also $\mathbb{P}$-possible, so the set of events with positive probability may be larger under $\mathbb{P}$. What matters for the capacity problem is that, on every event to which both measures assign positive probability, the channel law coincides, and thus the operational conclusion remains unchanged. However, Theorem 5 does not, by itself, certify that changes in $\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}$ exhaust the design space relevant to $\sup_{X[0,t]}$. A completeness statement would require a converse: any admissible change of propensities for reactions in $\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}^{C}$ alters the channel law $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y|X}$ on a set of positive measure. This appears natural in light of the graphical intuition developed above, but establishing the converse lies beyond the scope of this work.  

Finally, while we did neither stipulate a fully reversible CRN nor elementary reactions in the definition of the reversible SR-graph, we finally assess the consequences of thermodynamically consistent modeling on the set of encoding channels.  

Theorem 6. For any causal chemical communication model that is based on a reversible CRN it holds that  

$$

\mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f f } } = \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f b } }

$$  

if all reactions are elementary (and physically meaningful). In particular,  

$$

\mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } \setminus \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { d } } = \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { T x } } ^ { \circ } .

$$  

Proof. The necessary condition for a reversible reactions channel $\mathcal{R}_r$ to have unidirectional information flow is that it follows either of the structural assumptions depicted in Fig. 3. However, any effective enzymatic reaction is a non-elementary approximation of at least two elementary reactions. Hence, we can focus on the remaining case of bidirectional zero-order propensities in some species $d$ with $\pi_d(v_r) \neq 0$. The case of an elementary one-directional zero-order reaction, where the forward reaction does not depend on $d$, but the reverse reaction does, may arise from exchanges with a reservoir. That is, however, not a bidirectional zero-order case, which we deem not physically meaningful. In conclusion, unidirectional information flow does not happen in a reversible CRN consisting of elementary, physically meaningful reactions. Then, $\mathcal{R}_{\text{ff}} = \mathcal{R}_{\text{fb}}$ follows immediately and hence implies  

$$

\mathcal { R } _ { \leftarrow } = \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { d } } \sqcup \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { T x } } ^ { \circ } .

$$  

A major consequence is that variation of feedback is not a degree of freedom that can be used to increase the channel capacity of a thermodynamically consistent chemical communication model.  

# B. Information flow as an optimization principle  

Using information flow in chemical reaction networks as an optimization objective function yields proposals for the properties of optimized information processing structures or their  

inputs. These proposals can be used as testable predictions to evaluate the hypothesis that information flow is an emergent optimization objective of evolution. Alternatively, they can be used as principled design suggestions for engineering chemical reaction networks optimized for information transmission.  

In this work, we introduce trajectory-level information measures between subnetworks that extend earlier steady-state ensemble-level metrics [103] and Gaussian channel approximations [104]. By formulating the information measures on the paths probabilities of the underlying molecular dynamics, the framework captures the full temporal structure of the process. Thermodynamically consistent modeling enables the replacement of previous ad-hoc cost functions, which constrained mean molecular counts or their variances [103, 104], with energetic bounds derived from first principles. Taken together, these extensions allow the paradigm to be explored at a finer molecular-level and temporal resolution while remaining anchored in fundamental physical laws. Further, directed information and causally conditioned path measures add a notion of causal directionality to the mathematical formulation of the optimization principle, distinguishing causal forward information flow from causal feedback.  

The paradigm of information optimization in biological systems has largely been discussed using mutual information, often motivated by the argument that mutual information is a bias-free measure of statistical dependence and thus serves as a minimal requirement for the presence of information flow [6]. While this rationale supports mutual information as a suitable objective function, it does not capture the causal structure present in many information-processing tasks. Earlier work introduced predictive information as a trajectory-level measure quantifying how much the past output dynamics reveal about future input dynamics [105]. Although not always framed in explicitly directional terms, this formulation inherently distinguishes the predicted quantity (future input) from the given information (past output), implying a temporal and functional asymmetry. Directed information formalizes such asymmetries by explicitly accounting for causal direction, and thus serves as a natural extension of mutual information when distinguishing forward from feedback pathways is essential.  

Recalling Proposition 3 and adopting the previously discussed communication system perspective, the directed information $\mathbb{I}(X_{[0,t]} \rightarrow Y_{[0,t]})$ can be interpreted as an upper bound, and thus a proxy, for the information flow from an inaccessible external stimulus $\theta_{[0,t]}$ to the output $Y_{[0,t]}$, effectively excluding feedback contributions that would otherwise be included in the mutual information $\mathbb{I}(X_{[0,t]};Y_{[0,t]})$.  

Moreover, Definition 18 of encoding channels can be fruitfully applied in the information-optimization paradigm. It allows specific subsets of the reaction network to be held invariant, enabling the targeted design or analysis of other network components.  

Recall that the Janossy density, represented as a function of trajectories, admits the factorization in Eq. (65). Assuming a fixed initial distribution, then implies that fixing $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\parallel X}$ leaves $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel Y}$ as degrees of freedom for optimization, and vice versa. Consequently, optimizing over the causally conditioned dis  

tribution or the marginal path distribution is equivalent if only one of the causally conditional probabilities is held fixed. In this case, Theorem 5 applies equivalently, when $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel Y}$ is held invariant the encoding channels are replaced with  

$$

\mathcal { R } _ { \rightarrow } : = \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { d } } \cup \left[ \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f f } } \setminus \left( \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f b } } \cup \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { T x } } \right) \right] \cup \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { R x } } ^ { \circ } .

$$  

If, however, the marginal law $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X}$ is fixed, the situation becomes more difficult. Then, we must ensure that every change in the $Y_{[0,t]}$-dynamics is either "compensated for" or "not fed back into" the $X_{[0,t]}$-dynamics. If no change in $Y$ is fed back to $X$, then $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X}=\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel Y}$, which maps the optimization problem to the one, where the causal conditional probability is fixed. The case where compensation is required is beyond the scope of this work.  

Bialek [93] has summarized two mathematical approaches to test the hypothesis that evolutionary processes optimize information flow: (1) distribution matching and (2) network architecture. We extend these frameworks to the setting of path-level directed information subject to thermodynamic energy constraints.  

In the distribution matching framework, the input distribution is optimized for a given channel. This can be interpreted as an inverse problem to evolutionary network optimization: given the channel $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\parallel X}$, does the actual input distribution $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X}$ or the feedback-encoding distribution $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel Y}$ maximize forward information flow?  

To eliminate the degree of freedom associated with the initial distribution, we assume fixed initial conditions $X(0) = x(0) \in \mathcal{X}$ and $Y(0) = y(0) \in \mathcal{Y}$. The two equivalent formulations of the distribution matching problem are then  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \tilde { \mathbb { P } } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { X | Y } = \underset { X _ { [ 0 , t ] } | Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \arg \operatorname* { s u p } } ~ \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) , } \\ & { \tilde { \mathbb { P } } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { X } = \underset { X _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \arg \operatorname* { s u p } } ~ \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) , } \\ & { \mathrm { ~ s . ~ t . ~ } \quad \frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { E } \left[ q ( t ) \right] \leq \mathcal { Q } _ { 0 } , } \end{array}

$$  

if the optimal distribution exists. Otherwise the solution can be represented as a sequence of distributions achieving the optimum asymptotically. The supremum is taken under any physically principled constraint of the form (61), which can involve the energetic contributions of the entire network or only a subset.  

The optimization involves modifications such as rescaling or removal of encoding reactions $\mathcal{R}_{\leftarrow}$, consistent with Theorem 5. Since the path measures are fully determined by the network's propensity functions, optimization may be carried out directly over these function spaces. In this way, the distribution matching framework becomes one of "matching propensity functions (and possibly network topology) under energetic constraints". If $X_{[0,t]}$ is causally independent of $Y_{[0,t]}$, that is, $\mathbb{I}(Y_{[0,t]} \rightarrow X_{[0,t]}) = 0$, then mutual information equals directed information under fixed initial conditions. Similarly, if X models an external signal such as DNA-damaging photon arrival statistics, rather than a part of the CRN, then energetic  

constraints on $X_{[0,t]}$ are irrelevant, and kinetic constraints may be more appropriate.  

In contrast, the network architecture framework keeps the input process, or feedback encoding, fixed and optimizes the kinetic parameters and topology of the channel. This corresponds more directly to the evolutionary hypothesis, wherein the internal network evolves to optimally process a given environmental input. Again assuming fixed initial conditions and fixing $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X\parallel Y}$ (not $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{X}$), we consider the problems  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { P } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { Y | | X } = \underset { Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } | | X _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \arg \operatorname* { s u p } } ~ \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) , } \\ & { \mathbb { P } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ^ { Y } = \underset { Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } } { \arg \operatorname* { s u p } } ~ \mathbb { I } ( X _ { [ 0 , t ] } \rightarrow Y _ { [ 0 , t ] } ) , } \\ & { \mathrm { ~ s . ~ t . ~ } \quad \frac { 1 } { t } \mathbb { E } \left[ q ( t ) \right] \leq Q _ { 0 } , } \end{array}

$$  

where again the supremum may only be achieved asymptotically by a sequence of distributions. The resulting channel process, defined by its topology and propensity functions, yields a principled design proposal under varying energy budgets, or a testable prediction in support of the information-optimization hypothesis.  

Both frameworks can also be formulated in terms of the information rate (6). This information rate perspective may even be more appropriate for the discussed application, as it eliminates the effect of an arbitrarily chosen finite time-window. In the following, we illustrate this optimization framework in two small-scale, analytically tractable examples, where the capacity of biochemical channels is evaluated under energetic constraints using promoter-level models of transcriptional regulation.  

# C. Case studies: Accurate energy-accounting and information-dissipation trade-off in small promoter models  

We now apply the optimization principle to two analytically tractable promoter models. In each model the promoter state trajectory acts as the channel input $X$ and the mRNA transcription record as the output $Y$. Our task is to maximize the directed information rate $\mathbb{I}(X \rightarrow Y)$ subject to an upper bound on the promoter's entropy-production rate $e_p$. Transcription events are excluded from the thermodynamic budget because (i) their energetic cost is invariant for a fixed gene and does not affect the optimal promoter design, and (ii) the transcription events are not modeled thermodynamically consistent for simplicity and tractability. The resulting constrained problems are solved with fmincon (MATLAB R2022a), providing quantitative examples of the distribution-matching framework in which the input kinetics are tuned to exploit the full dynamic range of the transcriptional output [93].  

Consider a two-state Markov jump process with states OFF and ON, which modulate transcriptional activity by switching between the transcription rates 0 and $k_{\mathrm{tx}}$. For simplicity, we set $k_{\mathrm{tx}}=1$ in our analysis, thereby fixing the system's time scale. The state transition diagram of this model, along with transition rates and thermodynamic assumptions, is shown in Fig. 4. The model features a pair of parallel reaction channels: the first channel involves ATP hydrolysis with $\Delta \mu=\mu_{\mathrm{ADP}+\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{i}}}-\mu_{\mathrm{ATP}}<0$ and is coupled to corresponding chemostats, while the second reaction channel is thermally "driven" and not coupled to a chemostat. We denote the Gibbs free energy difference between the states by $\Delta g=g(\mathrm{ON})-g(\mathrm{OFF})$. The entropy production rate at the NESS is  

$$

e _ { p } = J \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } \right)

$$  

with net probability flux $J=\frac{k_{1}^{+}k_{2}^{-}-k_{2}^{+}k_{1}^{-}}{k_{1}^{+}+k_{1}^{-}+k_{2}^{+}+k_{2}^{-}}$, as we show in appendix F. This model provides a minimal example of the un  

derestimation of the true entropy production by the effective entropy production (cf. Section IVF), as the effective two-state model always satisfies detailed balance at stationarity, such that its effective entropy production rate vanishes.  

The three-state promoter model in Figure 5 extends the two-state model by adding an intermediate state, OFF*, which represents a step in a non-elementary reaction involving ATP hydrolysis. In this model, the first reaction channel handles ATP binding and unbinding between OFF and OFF*, while the second channel accounts for the actual ATP hydrolysis, driving the transition from OFF* to ON. The third reaction channel allows direct transitions between OFF and ON and is not coupled to any chemostat species. Distinguishing ATP binding/unbinding and hydrolysis may thereby be a more accurate account of the elementary reaction assumption than the single driven channel in the two-state model. Thermodynamically, the three-state promoter is characterized by  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \Delta _ { 3 } ^ { + } Q = - \, \mu _ { \mathrm { A D P + P _ { i } } } - \Delta _ { 3 } ^ { + } g = \frac { 1 } { \beta } \ln \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 3 } ^ { - } } , } \\ & { \Delta g : = \Delta _ { 2 } ^ { + } g = \Delta _ { 1 } ^ { + } g + \Delta _ { 3 } ^ { + } g \mathrm { ~ a n d } } \\ & { \Delta _ { 2 } ^ { - } Q = \Delta g = \frac { 1 } { \beta } \ln \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } . } \end{array}

$$  

The EPR satisfies  

$$

e _ { p } = J \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } \right)

$$  

with $J = k_{2}^{-}\pi(\mathrm{ON}) - k_{2}^{+}\pi(\mathrm{OFF})$, which is related to the general form of the EPR for circular state transition diagrams [81].  

We now formulate the maximization problem and use the (information) capacity notation for the maximum DIR. For the two-state promoter we compute  

$$

C _ { \mathbb { I } } ( \varepsilon _ { 0 } ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \substack { 0 \leq \overline { { k } } ^ { + } , \overline { { k } } ^ { - } \\ 0 \leq e _ { p } ( \overline { { k } } ^ { + } , \overline { { k } } ^ { - } ) \leq \varepsilon _ { 0 } } } \overline { { \mathbb { I } } } ( X \to Y )

$$  

where $\overline{k}^{+}=k_{1}^{+}+k_{2}^{+}$, $\overline{k}^{-}=k_{1}^{-}+k_{2}^{-}$ are the effective rates of the transitions between OFF and ON.  

The physical boundary conditions $-\Delta\mu,k_{1}^{+},k_{2}^{-}>0$ constrain the admissible region of the effective rates:  

$$

\overline { { k } } ^ { + } > \overline { { k } } ^ { - } \, e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } , \quad \overline { { k } } ^ { - } > \overline { { k } } ^ { + } \, e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) }

$$  

For this admissible region the EPR is thus expressed in terms of the effective rates via  

$$

e _ { p } ( \overline { { k } } ^ { + } , \overline { { k } } ^ { - } ) = - \beta \Delta \mu \frac { \left( \overline { { k } } ^ { + } - \overline { { k } } ^ { - } \, e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } \right) \left( \overline { { k } } ^ { - } \, e ^ { - \beta \Delta \mu } - \overline { { k } } ^ { + } \, e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } \right) } { \left( \overline { { k } } ^ { + } + \overline { { k } } ^ { - } \right) \left( e ^ { - \beta \Delta \mu } - 1 \right) } ,

$$  

as we show in Appendix F.  

The DIR for the model with a two-state promoter has been  

$$

\mid - \Delta g / \Delta \mu = 0 . 5

$$  

function of the upper bound $\varepsilon_0 \geq e_p$.  

(b) Density plot of $e_{p}$ together with its level sets (black lines) and the DIR-maximizing rates on the sets with $e_{p} \leq \varepsilon_{0}$ for varying $\varepsilon_{0} > 0$ (yellow line).  
shown to satisfy [39]  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { I } ( X \rightarrow Y ) = \frac { 1 } { \mathrm { E } [ \tau ] } ( \ln ( k _ { \mathrm { t x } } ) + h ( \tau ) - 1 ) } \\ & { \quad = k _ { \mathrm { t x } } \pi ( \mathrm { O N } ) ( \ln ( k _ { \mathrm { t x } } ) + h ( \tau ) - 1 ) } \\ & { \quad = k _ { \mathrm { t x } } \frac { h ( \tilde { \tau } ) - 1 } { \mathrm { E } [ \tilde { \tau } ] } , } \end{array}

$$  

where $h(\tau)$ is the differential entropy of the random time $\tau$ between transcription events in the dynamics of $Y$. $\tilde{\tau} = k_{\mathrm{tx}} \tau$ is its dimensionless version. The probability density of $\tau$ is  

$$

p ( \tau = t ) = \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } - w _ { 1 } } { w _ { 2 } - w _ { 1 } } w _ { 2 } e ^ { - w _ { 2 } t } + \frac { w _ { 2 } - k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { w _ { 2 } - w _ { 1 } } w _ { 1 } e ^ { - w _ { 1 } t } ,

$$  

which is an affine combination of exponentials and where $w_1, w_2 > 0$ are the roots of $w \mapsto (\overline{k}^- + k_{\text{tx}} - w)(\overline{k}^+ - w) - \overline{k}^+ \overline{k}^-$ with $w_1 < w_2$.  
keeping the ratio fixed. In Figure 6a the information capacity is evaluated for different $\varepsilon_{0} > 0$, given that $-\Delta g/\Delta \mu = 0.5$. Complementarily, Figure 6b displays a density plot of the EPR $e_{p}$ in the $(\bar{k}^{+}, \bar{k}^{-})$-plane together with (i) its hyperbolic level sets and (ii) the curve of DIR maximizing effective rates for varying $\varepsilon_{0} > 0$. The density plot illustrates that upper bounding the EPR does not upper bound the kinetic rates.  

In contrast, Figure 7 shows the information capacity for varying $-\Delta g/\Delta\mu$ and different level sets of $e_{p}$. Hence, we do not only vary the absolute ATP concentration, but also explore the maximum for a continuous range of possible core species identities. In the range $-\Delta g/\Delta\mu \in [0,1]$ the maximum DIR increases monotonously with increasing $e_{p}$.  

For $-\Delta g/\Delta\mu\notin[0,1]$ the level set $e_{p}=0$ in the $(\bar{k}^{+},\bar{k}^{-})$-plane always intersects with the DIR maximizing ratio $\bar{k}^{-}/\bar{k}^{+}\approx e-1$ [37]. However, $-\Delta g/\Delta\mu<0$ can be regarded as implausible since ON would then have less Gibbs free energy than OFF. $-\Delta g/\Delta\mu>1$ on the other hand corresponds to $\Delta_{1}^{+}Q<0$, i.e., a chemically driven reaction, which additionally absorbs heat from the environment. Both scenarios are biophysically implausible.  

For the three-state promoter let $X$ represent the subnetwork {ON}, i.e., the switching dynamics between a transcriptionally active and any inactive promoter state {OFF, OFF*}. We define the capacity problem as  

$$

C _ { \mathbb { I } } ( \varepsilon _ { 0 } ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \substack { 0 \leq k _ { 1 } ^ { + } , k _ { 1 } ^ { - } , k _ { 2 } ^ { - } , k _ { 3 } ^ { + } \\ 0 \leq e _ { p } \leq \varepsilon _ { 0 } } } \bar { \mathbb { I } } ( X \rightarrow Y ) ,

$$  

where $\Delta g$, $\Delta\mu$ and $k_{\mathrm{tx}}$ are fixed. The DIR of this model can be shown to have the form [39]  

$$

\mathbb { \bar { I } } ( X \rightarrow Y ) = k _ { \mathrm { t x } } \ln ( k _ { \mathrm { t x } } ) \pi ( \mathrm { O N } ) + \frac { h ( \tau ) - 1 } { \mathrm { E } [ \tau ] } .

$$  

The corresponding information capacity curves for $k_{\mathrm{tx}}=1$ in Figure 8 are analogous to the curves of the two-state promoter.  

In the light of information flow as an optimization principle, the two systems $U$ (one with two-state promoter and the other  

function of the upper bound $\varepsilon_0 \geq e_p$.  

(b) Maximum DIR as a function of $-\Delta g/\Delta\mu$ for different level sets of $e_{p}=\varepsilon_{0}$.  
with three-state promoter) hence, have the same information-dissipation trade-off. Note that the channel $\mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X}$ equals in the two models and the two optimizations (and their combination) can hence be regarded as small examples of the "distribution matching framework". The two distinct input topologies do not change the information capacity with respect to kinetic rate optimization under an entropy production rate constraint.  

# VII. CONCLUSION AND OUTLOOK  

This work establishes a trajectory-level framework that links information processing and thermodynamic cost in Markovian chemical reaction networks (CRNs). On the information-theoretic side, we formulate mutual information and directed information between continuous-time trajectories and extend these notions to information exchange and causal information flow between arbitrary subnetworks, clarifying when mutual information is ill-defined. On the thermodynamic side, we provide a thermodynamically consistent account for open CRNs in Kurtz's process formulation and highlight that, at stationarity, the entropy production rate identifies the mean dissipation rate, while parallel reaction channels can invalidate common coarse-grained identifications if not modeled carefully.  

Building on these foundations, we introduce, to the best of our knowledge, the first Shannon-style communication channel model for CRNs that explicitly separates message, encoding, and decoding and that permits principled energetic constraints on admissible codes. In this setting, the finite-time information capacity and its capacity–cost variants are formulated as optimizations over causal encoding laws subject to physical bounds (e.g., on dissipated heat or entropy production), and we provide a converse coding theorem under such constraints. A key structural contribution is the reaction-network classification that isolates design degrees of freedom from an invariant physical channel law. Modifying propensities within the class of encoding reactions preserves the causal channel kernel, whereas changes outside these classes generically alter it. Finally, analytically tractable promoter models illustrate how energetic constraints shape achievable information flow, yielding explicit information-dissipation trade-offs.  

Several limitations delineate immediate directions for future work. First, we do not prove a full channel coding theorem for the CRN channel class; accordingly, the identification of information capacity with operational capacity is made under an information-stability assumption, and we only provide a converse bound. Establishing sufficient conditions for information stability in chemically relevant regimes remains open. Second, while the channel model allows time-dependent message protocols, thermodynamic constraints for general time-dependent driving are not available in closed form at the same level of generality. Obtaining them requires a microscopic derivation from the underlying Hamiltonian and is beyond the present scope. Third, the present results are exact at the level of elementary reaction networks. For full gene-expression networks, even a single transcriptional module may correspond to thousands of elementary reactions, and experimental knowledge at this resolution can be limited. An important challenge is therefore to make the framework accessible for non-elementary (coarse-grained) descriptions without losing control of the relevant information and energetic quantities.  

Conceptually, we view the developed tools as a foundation for a reaction-network-based theory of signal processing: the principled design and analysis of biochemical signal encoding and processing under explicit causal, information-theoretic, and thermodynamic criteria. In this light, the reaction classification that enables a separation between "causal channel" and "causal (feedback) encoding" is best viewed as a tool for importing the classical noisy channel coding problem into the CRN setting. Thereby we have clarified what is meant by "communication through a CRN" and how this notion is invoked in an operational Shannon-sense, rather than a mere metaphorical one. We envision that, prospectively, our tools can serve as a starting point for progressing from the classical noisy channel coding problem, in which the channel is fixed, to more realistic biochemical coding problems. Natural next steps include extending the present methods to scenarios where parts of the "channel" are tunable (i.e., joint code-channel co-design) or performance metrics are goal-oriented, as well as developing controlled approximation schemes that bridge elementary and effective network descriptions.

# Appendix A: Chain rules for directed information  

The following chain rules for the Newton DI are derived similarly to the original proofs given by Kramer [67] for the Massey DI.  

Lemma 4. For the random discrete sequences $X_{1:N}$, $Y_{1:N}$ and $Z_{1:N}$ the DI satisfies the chain rules  

$$

\mathbb { I } \big ( X _ { 1 : N } , Z _ { 1 : N } \to Y _ { 1 : N } \big ) = \mathbb { I } \big ( X _ { 1 : N } \to Y _ { 1 : N } \big ) + \mathbb { I } \big ( Z _ { 1 : N } \to Y _ { 1 : N } | | X _ { 1 : N } \big )

$$  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { I } \left( X _ { 1 : N } \rightarrow Y _ { 1 : N } , Z _ { 1 : N } \right) = \mathbb { I } \left( X _ { 1 : N } \rightarrow Y _ { 1 : N } \big | \big | Z _ { 1 : N } \right) } \\ & { \qquad \qquad + \mathbb { I } \left( X _ { 1 : N } \rightarrow Z _ { 1 : N } \big | \big | D ^ { - 1 } Y _ { 1 : N } \right) , } \end{array}

$$  

where $D^{-1}$ denotes an anticipatory shift such that  

$$

\mathbb { I } \left( X _ { 1 : N } \rightarrow Z _ { 1 : N } | | D ^ { - 1 } Y _ { 1 : N } \right) = \sum _ { n = 2 } ^ { N } \mathbb { I } \left( X _ { 1 : n - 1 } ; Z _ { n } \mid Y _ { 1 : n } , Z _ { 1 : n - 1 } \right) .

$$
