# Appendix B: Stochastic filtering equations for the intensity processes  

In order to compute the MI or DI from the expressions in the Theorems 1 and 2, we need to evaluate the intensity processes $\lambda^{X}$, $\hat{\lambda}^{X}$, $\lambda^{Y}$ and $\hat{\lambda}^{Y}$. Computing the MI from similar expressions and approximations thereof has been discussed extensively in [35]. If, however, there are $(X,Y)$-, $X$- or $Y$-indistinguishable reactions, then this needs to be accounted for [69] (see also the discussion in Sec. III B). To improve accessibility, we provide the relevant expressions (Eq. (21) and (22) in [69]) in our notation.  

We maintain all assumptions from Sec. III B. Let $\mathcal{I}_A \subset \mathcal{I}$ be an arbitrary subset of species indices of the core system U, and its complement $\mathcal{I}_B := \mathcal{I} \setminus \mathcal{I}_A$. The set $\mathcal{I}_A$ represents the set of species with respect to whom's history we want to filter the propensity functions in Eq (15). Hence, we choose $\mathcal{I}_A \in \{\mathcal{I}_X, \mathcal{I}_Y, \mathcal{I}_X \cup \mathcal{I}_Y\}$ in this context. Define $A := \{U_d(s): d \in \mathcal{I}_A\}_{s \geq 0}$ and $B := \{U_d(s): d \in \mathcal{I}_B\}_{s \geq 0}$, together with the natural filtration $\mathcal{F}^A$, as well as the coordinate maps $\pi_A$, $\pi_B$ and the non-zero subnetwork change vectors $\gamma_A$, $\gamma_B$. In the following we substitute $U(s) = (A(s), B(s))$.  

Exemplary, we want to evaluate the $\mathcal{F}^{A}$-intensity process of the reaction counter $R^{X}(v_{X})$, $v_{X} \in \mathcal{V}_{X}$:  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { X } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \big | \mathcal { F } _ { s - } ^ { A } \right] } \\ & { = \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { X } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } , A ( s ^ { - } ) , B ( s ^ { - } ) ) \big | \mathcal { F } _ { s - } ^ { A } \right] } \\ & { = \sum _ { b } \Pi _ { b } ( s ^ { - } ) \, \lambda ^ { X } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } , A ( s ^ { - } ) , b ) } \end{array}

$$  

for all $s \in [0, t]$, where $\Pi_b(s)$ denotes the conditional probability mass function $s \mapsto \Pi_b(s) := \mathbb{P}\left(B(s) = b \big| \mathcal{F}_s^A\right)$ as a function of time, which is actually a piecewise-deterministic stochastic process on the state space $[0, 1]$ for all $b \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{|\mathcal{S}_B|}$. We refer to $\{\Pi_b(s), b \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{|\mathcal{S}_B|}\}$ as the filtering distribution at time $s \geq 0$. It follows the stochastic differential equation  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathrm { d } \Pi _ { b } ( s ) = \left( - \lambda ^ { A } ( 0 , A ( s ^ { - } ) , b ) \, \Pi _ { b } ( s ^ { - } ) + \displaystyle \sum _ { \nu \in \pi _ { A } ^ { - 1 } ( 0 ) \cap \pi _ { B } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathcal { V } _ { B } ) } \lambda _ { \mathcal { V } ^ { - 1 } ( \nu ) } \big ( A ( s ^ { - } ) , b - \pi _ { B } ( \nu ) \big ) \, \Pi _ { b - \pi _ { B } ( \nu ) } ( s ^ { - } ) \right) \, \mathrm { d } s } \\ & { \quad - \displaystyle \sum _ { \nu _ { A } \in \mathcal { V } _ { A } } \left( \lambda ^ { A } ( \nu _ { A } , A ( s ^ { - } ) , b ) - \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { A } ( \nu _ { A } ) \right) \, \Pi _ { b } ( s ^ { - } ) \, \mathrm { d } s } \\ & { \quad + \displaystyle \sum _ { \nu _ { A } \in \mathcal { V } _ { A } } \left( \frac { \sum _ { \nu \in \pi _ { A } ^ { - 1 } ( \nu _ { A } ) } \lambda _ { \mathcal { V } ^ { - 1 } ( \nu ) } \big ( A ( s ^ { - } ) , b - \pi _ { B } ( \nu ) \big ) \, \Pi _ { b - \pi _ { B } ( \nu ) } ( s ^ { - } ) } { \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { A } ( \nu _ { A } ) } - \Pi _ { b } ( s ^ { - } ) \right) \, \mathrm { d } R _ { s } ^ { A } ( \nu _ { A } ) } \end{array}

$$  

such that $\Pi_{b}(s)=\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{\~d}\Pi_{b}(u)$, where  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \lambda ^ { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } , a , b ) : = \displaystyle \sum _ { \mathbf { v } \in \pi _ { A } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } ) } \lambda _ { V ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { v } ) } ( a , b ) } \\ & { \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } ) : = \! \! \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } , A ( s ^ { - } ) , B ( s ^ { - } ) ) \big | \mathcal { F } _ { s ^ { - } } ^ { A } \right] } \\ & { \qquad = \! \! \sum _ { b } \! \! \lambda ^ { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } , A ( s ^ { - } ) , b ) \, \Pi _ { b } ( s ^ { - } ) } \end{array}

$$  

Importantly, the stochastic updates at jumps of the counters $R^{A}(\nu_{A})$, $\nu_{A} \in \mathcal{V}_{A}$ depend on the correct conditioning. Using the reaction counters $R_{r}, r \in \mathcal{R}^{X}$ (cf. Eq. (24)) instead would add the surplus information, which of some X-indistinguishable reaction has happened. In particular, the enumerator and denominator of the update equation satisfy  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \sum _ { v \in \pi _ { A } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } ) } \lambda _ { V ^ { - 1 } ( v ) } ( A ( s ^ { - } ) , b - \pi _ { B } ( v ) ) \Pi _ { b - \pi _ { B } ( v ) } ( s ^ { - } ) } \\ { = } & { \sum _ { r \in V ^ { - 1 } ( \pi _ { A } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } ) ) } \lambda _ { r } ( A ( s ^ { - } ) , b - \pi _ { B } ( v _ { r } ) ) \Pi _ { b - \pi _ { B } ( v _ { m } ) } ( s ^ { - } ) } \\ { \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } ) = } & { \sum _ { r \in V ^ { - 1 } ( \pi _ { A } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { v } _ { A } ) ) } \sum _ { b } \lambda _ { r } ( A ( s ^ { - } ) , b ) \Pi _ { b } ( s ^ { - } ) } \end{array}

$$  

with $V^{-1}(\pi_A^{-1}(\nu_A)) \subseteq \mathcal{R}^X$ being a set of $X$-indistinguishable reaction indices. The continuous part of the evolution equation does not depend on the choice of conditioning, discussed here [69].  

# Appendix C: Transfer entropy between subnetworks and causally conditioned probabilities  

While the primary focus of this paper is on mutual and directed information, we also provide an expression for the transfer entropy between subnetworks to facilitate comparison for an interdisciplinary readership. Following conventional notation, we refer to $X$ as the target and $Y$ as the source. To accommodate the definition of transfer entropy, we extend the definition of stochastic processes from the finite interval $[0,t]$ to the real line $\mathbb{R}$, such that the CRN is assumed to have started at time $-\infty$ rather than at time zero.  

Consider the families of sigma-algebras {$\mathcal{G}_s^{XY,(\alpha,\beta)}$}$^{s\in\mathbb{R}}$ and {$\mathcal{G}_s^{X,(\alpha)}$}$^{s\in\mathbb{R}}$, defined by  

$$

\mathcal { G } _ { s } ^ { X Y , ( \alpha , \beta ) } : = \pmb { \sigma } \big ( X ( \tau ) , \tau \in [ s - \alpha , s ] \big ) \vee \pmb { \sigma } \big ( Y ( \tau ) , \tau \in [ s - \beta , s ] \big ) ,

$$  

$$

\mathcal { G } _ { s } ^ { X , ( \alpha ) } : = \pmb { \sigma } \left( X ( \tau ) , \tau \in [ s - \alpha , s ] \right) ,

$$  

which represent the sliding-window partial histories of the joint process $(X,Y)$ and the marginal process $X$, respectively. The parameters $\alpha,\beta>0$ specify the history lengths for $X$ and $Y$. Note that these families are generally not filtrations, since the fixed-length window definitions imply $\mathcal{G}_s^{XY,(\alpha,\beta)}\not\subset\mathcal{G}_{s'}^{XY,(\alpha,\beta)}$ and $\mathcal{G}_s^{X,(\alpha)}\not\subset\mathcal{G}_{s'}^{X,(\alpha)}$ for $s<s'$ in general. They become filtrations only in the limit $\alpha,\beta\to\infty$.  

Spinney et al. [36] generalize the discrete-time definition of transfer entropy to arbitrary stochastic processes indexed by a strictly ordered and uncountable set $\mathbb{T}$. For continuous-time processes, they assume $\mathbb{T} \supseteq [t_0 - \max(\alpha, \beta), t)$ with $t_0 < t$. Their approach constructs a regular version of the probability measure  

$$

\mathbb { P } \left( X _ { A } \in \mathcal { A } \Big | \left\{ \mathcal { G } _ { s } ^ { X Y , ( \alpha , \beta ) } \right\} _ { s \in A } \right) : = \prod _ { s \in A } \mathbb { P } \left( X ( s ) \in \mathcal { A } \Big | \mathcal { G } _ { s - } ^ { X Y , ( \alpha , \beta ) } \right)

$$  

for every finite subset $A \subset [t_0, t)$ and every product set $\mathcal{A}_A = \bigtimes_{s \in A} \mathcal{A}_s \in \Sigma_X^{\otimes A}$. This family of finite-dimensional distributions determines a unique probability measure on the path space over $[t_0, t)$ by Kolmogorov's Extension Theorem. The notation $\mathbb{P}(\cdot \|\cdot)$ is adopted from the "causally conditioned probability" as originally defined by Kramer [107]. Note that the definition in (C1) can be applied for arbitrary families of filtrations. Consequently, a causally conditioned probability for continuous-time systems, generalizing Kramers definition in the sense of Newton, is given by $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t)}^X \left( \cdot \|\left\{ \mathcal{F}_s^{XY} \right\}_{s \in [0,t)} \right)$ with short notation $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t)}^{X \parallel Y}$.  

The transfer entropy is then defined measure-theoretically via the Radon-Nikodym derivative of this probability measure with respect to the conditional probability, i.e.,  

$$

T _ { y \to x } ^ { ( \alpha , \beta ) } | _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { t } : = \mathbb { E } \left[ \ln \frac { \mathsf { d } \mathbb { P } _ { [ t _ { 0 } , t ) } ^ { X } \left( \cdot \Big | \big \{ \mathcal { G } _ { s } ^ { X Y , ( \alpha , \beta ) } \big \} _ { s \in [ t _ { 0 } , t ) } \right) } { \mathsf { d } \mathbb { P } _ { [ t _ { 0 } , t ) } ^ { X } \left( \cdot \Big | \big \{ \mathcal { G } _ { s } ^ { X , ( \alpha ) } \big \} _ { s \in [ t _ { 0 } , t ) } \right) } \right] .

$$  

This framework also permits a pathwise, random-variable ver  

sion of transfer entropy, as is common in stochastic thermodynamics.  

For comparison, we provide a related generalization of Schreiber's discrete-time transfer entropy [108] based on the structural, extremal-based approach of Weissman et al. [12]  

$$

T _ { y \to x } ^ { ( \alpha , \beta ) } | _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { t } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { t \in \mathcal { T } ( t _ { 0 } , t ) } \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { n ( t ) } \mathbb { I } \left( Y _ { [ t _ { i - 1 } - \beta , t _ { i - 1 } ) } ; X _ { [ t _ { i - 1 } , t _ { i } ) } \mid X _ { [ t _ { i - 1 } - \alpha , t _ { i - 1 } ) } \right) .

$$  

Here, we simply conjecture that the two definitions are equivalent for sufficiently regular path spaces. Using the same approach outlined in Supp. Sec. S1.3, one can show that the latter definition yields the same expression for the transfer entropy of jump processes as that derived by Spinney et al. [36].  

Using the subnetwork propensities (15) we define the conditional intensity processes  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \lambda _ { s } ^ { X , ( \alpha , \beta ) } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) \! = \! \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { X } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \mid \mathcal { G } _ { s - } ^ { X Y , ( \alpha , \beta ) } \right] } \\ & { \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { X , ( \alpha ) } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) \! = \! \mathbb { E } \left[ \lambda ^ { X } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } , U ( s ^ { - } ) ) \mid \mathcal { G } _ { s - } ^ { X , ( \alpha ) } \right] . } \end{array}

$$  

Then we obtain  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { T _ { y \rightarrow x } ^ { ( \alpha , \beta ) } | _ { t _ { 0 } } = \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { t } \sum _ { \mathbf { v } _ { X } \in \mathcal { V } _ { X } } \mathbb { E } \left[ \phi \left( \lambda _ { s } ^ { X , ( \alpha , \beta ) } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) \right) \right] } \\ & { \qquad - \mathbb { E } \left[ \phi \left( \hat { \lambda } _ { s } ^ { X , ( \alpha ) } ( \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) \right) \right] \mathrm { d } s . } \end{array}

$$  

In the limit of full history this yields the relation  

$$

\operatorname* { l i m } _ { \alpha , \beta \to \infty } T _ { y \to x } ^ { ( \alpha , \beta ) } | _ { t _ { 0 } } = \mathbb { I } ( Y _ { ( - \infty , t ) } \to X _ { [ t _ { 0 } , t ) } \mid X _ { ( \infty , t _ { 0 } ) } ) .

$$  

There are two ways to make the transfer entropy equal to the DI. Either artificially set the path of $U$ to a constant for all $s < 0$ and randomly select $U(0)$. Then  

$$

\operatorname* { l i m } _ { \alpha , \beta \to \infty } T _ { y \to x } ^ { ( \alpha , \beta ) } | _ { 0 } = \mathbb { I } ( Y _ { [ 0 , t ) } \to X _ { [ 0 , t ) } ) .

$$  

Alternatively, the parameters $\alpha, \beta$ can be interpreted as time-dependent functions $\alpha(s), \beta(s)$. Setting $\alpha(s) = \beta(s) = s$ we obtain $\mathcal{G}_s^{XY,(s,s)} = \mathcal{F}_s^{XY}$ for all $s \geq 0$, such that  

$$

\mathbb { I } ( Y _ { [ 0 , t ) } \to X _ { [ 0 , t ) } ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } T _ { y \to x } ^ { ( s , s ) } ( s ) \, \mathrm { d } s .

$$

Thereby, we ignore trails between species sets and cycles. In total, there are 16 distinct configurations. For example,  

$$

\left( \begin{array} { c c } { { 1 } } & { { } } \\ { { 0 } } & { { 0 } } \\ { { 0 } } & { { } } \end{array} \right)

indicates that a trail from $\mathcal{S}_X$ to $\Re_r$ ($\mathcal{S}_X \rightarrow \Re_r$) exists, but neither a trail in the reverse direction nor any trail between $\Re_r$ and $\mathcal{S}_Y$ exists. Hence, for the given example, the connectivity via trails may be visualized as  

$$

\mathcal { S } _ { X } \rightleftarrows \Re _ { r } \nleq \mathcal { S } _ { Y } ,

$$  

where we use $\rightarrow$ instead of $\rightarrow$, since we reserve the latter arrowhead for the local (in)dependence notation.  

Then, for categories (a) - (d), we obtain  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } { \mathcal { R } _ { \mathrm { f f } } \colon } & { \left( \begin{array} { l l } { 1 } & \\ { 1 } & { 1 } \end{array} \right) , \left( \begin{array} { l l } { 1 } & \\ { 0 } & { Note that $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{ff}}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{fb}}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\circ}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{d}}$. For the intersection between feedforward and feedback, i.e., $\mathcal{I}_{X}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{R}_{r}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{I}_{Y}$, as well as the input and output modulating cases, we identify

# Appendix E: Formal definition of chemical channel codes  

We may formalize the notions of the chemical channel coding problem. The key technical point in our setting is that a change of input intensities typically induces a change of the underlying path measure. Hence, admissible encoders should be defined as pathwise (measure-free) objects, and only subsequently be related to probability measures via a consistency requirement.  

Throughout, fix a transmission duration $t > 0$ and write $\mu$ for Lebesgue measure on $[0,t]$. Let $\mathcal{D}([0,t],\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})$ denote the space of càdlàg trajectories equipped with its Borel $\sigma$-field. For $s \in [0,t]$, denote by $\mathrm{r}_s$ the restriction operator $\mathrm{r}_s(z) := z_{[0,s)}$. For a path $z \in \mathcal{D}([0,t],\cdot)$ let $\mathbb{J}(z) := \{s \in (0,t] : z(s) \neq z(s-)\}$ be its set of jump times. We write $L^1([0,t],[0,\infty))$ for integrable functions modulo $\mu$-a.e. equality [109], and use the natural restriction map $\mathrm{r}_s : L^1([0,t],[0,\infty)) \to L^1([0,s],[0,\infty))$ induced by restricting representatives.  

Let $\Pi \subseteq \mathcal{D}([0,t],\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{N})$ be a set of admissible protocols, for some fixed $N \in \mathbb{N}$, viewed as deterministic control trajectories on $[0,t]$. We assume that every $\Gamma \in \Pi$ is bounded and piecewise differentiable, and has càdlàg sample paths on $[0,t]$. For $s \in [0,t]$ we write $\Pi|_{[0,s)} := \{\text{r}_s(\Gamma) \mid \Gamma \in \Pi\}$.  

A (feedback) channel encoder will be defined as an intensity-process map into $L^{1}([0,t],[0,\infty))$; this encodes exactly the degree of identification relevant for compensators and likelihoods (integration against $\mu$), without reference to any ambient probability measure. Predictability and sample-path regularity are imposed deterministically via the existence of suitable representatives.  

Definition 20. A $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-code $\mathbb{C}$ for the message $M$ and the causal chemical channel $(\mathcal{I}_X,\mathbb{P}^Y\|_X,\mathcal{I}_Y)$ consists of the following objects.  

(a) For any protocol book $\Pi$ a noiseless source code is a bi-jective mapping  

$$

\mathsf { c } \colon \mathcal { M } \to \Pi .

$$  

(b) A channel initialization is a probability mass function $p_0\colon\mathcal{U}\to[0,1]$.  

(c) Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a source code with protocol book $\Pi$. A feedback channel encoder is a collection  

$$

\mathbf { f } : = \left( \mathbf { f } _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } \right) _ { \varepsilon \in \{ + , - \} , v _ { X } \in \mathcal { V } _ { X } }

$$  

of maps  

$$

\mathsf { f } _ { \varepsilon , \mathsf { v } _ { X } } : \Pi \times \mathcal { D } ( [ 0 , t ] , \mathcal { X } \times \mathcal { Y } ) \to L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , t ] , [ 0 , \infty ) ) .

$$  

These maps are required to be predictable in the following sense:  

For every $s \in [0,t]$ and all $\Gamma, \Gamma' \in \Pi$ and $(x,y), (x',y') \in \mathcal{D}([0,t], \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{Y})$,  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { r } _ { s } ( \Gamma ) = \mathbf { r } _ { s } ( \Gamma ^ { \prime } ) \ a n d \ \mathbf { r } _ { s } ( x , y ) = \mathbf { r } _ { s } ( x ^ { \prime } , y ^ { \prime } ) } \\ & { \implies \mathbf { r } _ { s } \big ( \mathbf { f } _ { \varepsilon , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ( \Gamma , ( x , y ) ) \big ) = \mathbf { r } _ { s } \big ( \mathbf { f } _ { \varepsilon , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ( \Gamma ^ { \prime } , ( x ^ { \prime } , y ^ { \prime } ) ) \big ) , } \end{array}

$$  

as elements of $L^{1}([0,s),[0,\infty))$. Moreover, for each $(\varepsilon,\mathbf{y}_{X})$ there exists a representative family (a "predictable regenerative version")  

$$

\Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } ^ { X } : [ 0 , t ] \times \Pi \times \mathcal { D } ( [ 0 , t ] , \mathcal { X } \times \mathcal { Y } ) \to [ 0 , \infty )

$$  

such that for all $(\Gamma,(x,y))$,  

$$

\Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } ^ { X } ( \cdot , \Gamma , ( x , y ) ) \in \mathfrak { f } _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } ( \Gamma , ( x , y ) ) ,

$$  

where $s\mapsto\Lambda_{\varepsilon,\mathrm{vX}}^{x}(s,\Gamma,(x,y))$ is càglàd on $[0,t]$ and satisfies the (regenerative) jump-time constraint  

$$

\mathbb { J } \Big ( \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } ^ { X } ( \cdot , \Gamma , ( x , y ) ) \Big ) \subseteq \mathbb { J } ( \Gamma ) \cup \mathbb { J } ( x ) \cup \mathbb { J } ( y ) .

$$  

(d) A feedback source-channel encoder is a collection  

$$

\mathbf { f } : = \left( \mathbf { f } _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } \right) _ { \varepsilon \in \{ + , - \} , v _ { X } \in \mathcal { V } _ { X } }

$$  

of maps  

$$

\mathsf { f } _ { \varepsilon , \mathsf { v } _ { X } } : \mathcal { M } \times \mathcal { D } ( [ 0 , t ] , \mathcal { X } \times \mathcal { Y } ) \to L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , t ] , [ 0 , \infty ) ) ,

$$  

satisfying the analogous deterministic predictability condition (with $m \in \mathcal{M}$ in place of $\Gamma$) and admitting càglàd representatives $s \mapsto \Lambda_{\varepsilon, \nu_X}^{M \to X}(s, m, (x, y))$ whose jump times obey  

$$

\mathbb { J } \Big ( \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , v _ { X } } ^ { M \to X } ( \cdot , m , ( x , y ) ) \Big ) \subseteq \mathbb { J } ( x ) \cup \mathbb { J } ( y ) .

$$  

(e) A channel decoder is a mapping  

$$

D \colon \mathcal { D } ( [ 0 , t ] , \mathcal { Y } ) \to \mathcal { M } \cup \{ 0 \} .

$$  

(f) A code $\mathbb{C} = (\mathbf{c}, p_0, \mathbf{f}, D)$, where $\mathbf{f}$ is a channel encoder, is called a split source-channel code. A code $\mathbb{C} = (p_0, \mathbf{f}, D)$, where $\mathbf{f}$ is a source-channel encoder, is called a source-channel code.  

Note that the variation of $\mathcal{V}_{X}$ is explicitly allowed. However, the presented definition need not be consistent with all the requirements of the Markovian CRN $U \mid M$. This level of detail is lost due to projection and marginalization to $(M, \theta, X, Y)$. Therefore, while we have just defined the general functional class of codes, not all of those codes can necessarily be realized via a finite CRN. The question, which codes can be realized with such CRNs is addressed in Sec. VI A 5.  

The following definition relates the code design to its induced probability law for the noisy channel coding problem.  

Definition 21. Let $\mathbb{C}$ be a split source-channel code for the chemical channel $(\mathcal{S}_X, \mathbb{P}^{Y\parallel X}, \mathcal{S}_Y)$. A probability measure $\mathbb{P}^{\mathbb{C}}$ on $(\Omega, \mathcal{F})$ is said to be consistent with $\mathbb{C}$ if:  

(i) $\mathbb{P}^{\mathbb{C}}(U(0)=u)=p_{0}(u)$ for all $u\in\mathcal{U}$;  

(ii) the protocol-valued process $\theta$ satisfies $\theta = c(M)$;  

(iii) for each $(\varepsilon,\nu_X)$, the reaction-counting process $R^X(\varepsilon\nu_X)$ admits an $\mathcal{F}^{\theta XY}$ - intensity process $\lambda^X(\varepsilon\nu_X)$ under $\mathbb{P}^C$, which satisfies  

$$

\lambda _ { s } ^ { X } ( \varepsilon \mathbf { v } _ { X } ) = \Lambda _ { \varepsilon , \mathbf { v } _ { X } } ^ { X } \big ( s , \pmb { \theta } , ( X , Y ) _ { [ 0 , t ] } \big ) , \qquad ( \mu \otimes \mathbb { P } ^ { \mathbb { C } } ) \mathrm { - } a . e .

$$  

on $([0,t]\times\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,t]}^{\theta XY}))$ for the "predictable regenerative version" $\Lambda_{\varepsilon,v_X}^X$ of $\mathfrak{f}_{\varepsilon,v_X}$ (as in Def. 20 (c));  

(iv) $\mathbb{P}^{\mathbb{C}} \ll \mathbb{P}$ and $\mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{CY\|X} = \mathbb{P}_{[0,t]}^{Y\|X}$, $\mathbb{P}^{\mathbb{C}}$-a.s. (where $\mathbb{P}$ acts as a reference measure).  

Consistency for a source-channel code C = (p_0, f, D) is defined analogously, with $\Lambda_{\varepsilon, v_X}^{M \rightarrow X}(s, M, (X,Y)_{[0,t]})$ in place of $\Lambda_{\varepsilon, v_X}^{X}(s, \theta, (X,Y)_{[0,t]})$ and $\mathcal{F}_{s^-}^{MXY}$-predictability.  

Definition 22. The set $\mathcal{C}$ of all $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-codes $\mathbb{C}$ for which there exists at least one consistent measure $\mathbb{P}^{\mathbb{C}}$ is called the set of $(|\mathcal{M}|,t)$-codes admissible for the causal chemical channel $(\mathcal{S}_X,\mathbb{P}^{Y|X},\mathcal{S}_Y)$.  

Note that $\mathcal{C}$ is not necessarily a product set of admissible initializations, encoders and decoders.  

# Appendix F: EPR of a non-equilibrium two-state model  

To derive the EPR for the two-state model we use Equation (47) for the entropy exchange rate, which is equal to the EPR at the NESS:  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { e _ { p } \! = \! \pi ( O F F ) k _ { 1 } ^ { + } \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } \right) + \pi ( O N ) k _ { 1 } ^ { - } \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } } \right) } \\ & { \quad + \pi ( O F F ) k _ { 2 } ^ { + } \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } \right) + \pi ( O N ) k _ { 2 } ^ { - } \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } \right) } \\ & { \quad = \! \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } \right) \left( \pi ( O F F ) k _ { 1 } ^ { + } - \pi ( O N ) k _ { 1 } ^ { - } \right) } \\ & { \quad \quad + \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } \right) \left( \pi ( O F F ) k _ { 2 } ^ { + } - \pi ( O N ) k _ { 2 } ^ { - } \right) } \\ & { \quad = \! \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } \right) \frac { ( k _ { 1 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { - } ) k _ { 1 } ^ { + } - ( k _ { 1 } ^ { + } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } ) k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } + k _ { 1 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } + k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } } \\ & { \quad \quad + \ln \left( \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } \right) \frac { ( k _ { 1 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { - } ) k _ { 2 } ^ { + } - ( k _ { 1 } ^ { + } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } ) k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } + k _ { 1 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } + k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } } \end{array}

$$  

with $\pi(OFF)=\frac{k_{1}^{-}+k_{2}^{-}}{k_{1}^{+}+k_{1}^{-}+k_{2}^{+}+k_{2}^{-}}$ and $\pi(ON)=\frac{k_{1}^{+}+k_{2}^{+}}{k_{1}^{+}+k_{1}^{-}+k_{2}^{+}+k_{2}^{-}}$. The stationary distribution is the kernel of the generator matrix corresponding to the model. Simplifying the above expression yields (66).  

To obtain the EPR as a function of the effective rates $\bar{k}^{+}=k_{1}^{+}+k_{2}^{+}$, $\bar{k}^{-}=k_{1}^{-}+k_{2}^{-}$, we we express the rates in the state transition diagram via the effective rates and the potential differences $\Delta g$, $\Delta\mu$. We have  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } = k _ { 1 } ^ { + } e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) } , \quad k _ { 2 } ^ { + } = k _ { 2 } ^ { - } e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } , } \\ & { \overline { { k } } ^ { - } = k _ { 1 } ^ { + } e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) } + k _ { 2 } ^ { - } , \quad \overline { { k } } ^ { + } = k _ { 1 } ^ { + } + k _ { 2 } ^ { - } e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } , } \end{array}

$$  

which implies  

$$

k _ { 1 } ^ { + } = \frac { \overline { { k } } ^ { + } - \overline { { k } } ^ { - } e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } } { 1 - e ^ { \Delta \mu } } , \quad k _ { 2 } ^ { - } = \frac { \overline { { k } } ^ { - } - \overline { { k } } ^ { + } e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) } } { 1 - e ^ { \Delta \mu } }

$$  

and therefore also  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } = \frac { \overline { { k } } ^ { + } - \overline { { k } } ^ { - } e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } } { 1 - e ^ { \Delta \mu } } e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) } } \\ { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } = \frac { \overline { { k } } ^ { - } - \overline { { k } } ^ { + } e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) } } { 1 - e ^ { \Delta \mu } } e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } } \end{array}

$$  

The physical boundary conditions $-\Delta\mu,k_{1}^{+},k_{2}^{-}>0$ constrain the admissible region of the effective rates to  

$$

\overline { { k } } ^ { + } > \overline { { k } } ^ { - } e ^ { - \beta \Delta g } , \quad \overline { { k } } ^ { - } > \overline { { k } } ^ { + } e ^ { \beta ( \Delta g + \Delta \mu ) } .

$$  

For this region we obtain (68) by substituting the original rates with the above expressions and simplifying.  

# Appendix G: Interarrival time between transcription events for the circular three-state promoter  

The DI between the promoter dynamics and the RNA copy number has was linked to the differential entropy of the interarrival time in (69). Here, we apply Anderson's filtering theorem for semi Markov processes similarly as described in [39]. Define the state space $E := \{\text{J}, \text{ON}, \text{OFF}^*, \text{OFF}\}$, where J is the jump state, which mimics ON in terms of leaving transitions. The Laplace version of the semi-Markov kernel density is  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { \boldsymbol { q } ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( \begin{array} { l l l l } { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } } \\ { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } } \\ { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } } { u _ { O F F ^ { * } } + s } } & { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O F F ^ { * } } + s } } \\ { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } { u _ { O F F } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } } { u _ { O F F } + s } } & { 0 } \end{array} \right) , } \end{array}

$$  

where $u_{ON} = k_{2}^{-} + k_{3}^{-} + k_{\mathrm{tx}}$, $u_{OFF} = k_{1}^{+} + k_{2}^{+}$ We filter $E$ to the reduced state space $\{\mathbf{J}\}$ which yields the interarrival time distribution $f_{\tau}$ of the output. Since the geometric series of a $3 \times 3$-matrix is hard to evaluate, we do the filtering state by  

state, from right to left. In the first step we identify  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \boldsymbol { a } ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( \begin{array} { c c c } { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } } \\ { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } } \\ { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } } { u _ { O F F ^ { * } } + s } } & { 0 } \end{array} \right) } \\ & { \boldsymbol { b } ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } , \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O N } + s } , \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } { u _ { O F F ^ { * } } + s } \right) ^ { T } , } \\ & { \boldsymbol { c } ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( 0 , \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } { u _ { O F F } + s } , \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } } { u _ { O F F } + s } \right) \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } } \\ & { \boldsymbol { d } ^ { * } = \boldsymbol { 0 } . } \end{array}

$$  

Thus  

$$

\begin{array} { r } { b ^ { * } ( s ) \, c ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( \begin{array} { l l } { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } \, \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } } \\ { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } \, \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } } \\ { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } k _ { 2 } ^ { + } } { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } \, \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } } \end{array} \right) } \end{array}

$$  

and  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \tilde { q } ^ { * } ( s ) = a ^ { * } ( s ) + b ^ { * } ( s ) \, c ^ { * } ( s ) } \\ & { = \left( \begin{array} { l l l } { \frac { k _ { 1 x } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } \, k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } \, k _ { 2 } ^ { - } + k _ { 3 } ^ { - } ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } } \\ { \frac { k _ { 1 x } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } \, k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } \, k _ { 2 } ^ { - } + k _ { 3 } ^ { - } ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } } \\ { 0 } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { - } \, k _ { 2 } ^ { + } + k _ { 3 } ^ { + } ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } } & { \frac { k _ { 1 } ^ { + } \, k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } } \end{array} \right) } \end{array}

$$  

In the second step we identify  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { a ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } , \frac { k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } \right) , } \\ & { b ^ { * } ( s ) = \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { - } \left( r _ { 2 } + s \right) } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) } \left( 1 , 1 \right) ^ { T } , } \\ & { c ^ { * } ( s ) = \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } \left( r _ { 1 } + s \right) } { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } \left( 0 , 1 \right) , } \\ & { \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } ( d ^ { * } ( s ) ) ^ { k } = \frac { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) } { ( u _ { O F F _ { * } } + s ) ( u _ { O F F } + s ) - k _ { 1 } ^ { + } k _ { 1 } ^ { - } } } \end{array}

$$

with $r_{1}:=\frac{k_{1}^{-}k_{2}^{+}}{k_{3}^{+}}+u_{OFF}$ and $r_{2}:=\frac{k_{1}^{+}k_{2}^{-}}{k_{3}^{-}}+u_{OFF}$. We denote the roots of $s\mapsto(u_{OFF*}-s)(u_{OFF}-s)-k_{1}^{+}k_{1}^{-}$ by $w_{1},w_{2}$. Then  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { b ^ { * } ( s ) \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } ( d ^ { * } ( s ) ) ^ { k } c ^ { * } ( s ) } \\ & { = \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } \left( r _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( r _ { 2 } + s \right) } { \left( u _ { O F F } + s \right) \left( u _ { O N } + s \right) \left( w _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( w _ { 2 } + s \right) } \left( \begin{array} { l l } { 0 } & { 1 } \\ { 0 } & { 1 } \end{array} \right) } \\ & { \tilde { q } ^ { * } ( s ) = a ^ { * } ( s ) + b ^ { * } ( s ) \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } ( d ^ { * } ( s ) ) ^ { k } c ^ { * } ( s ) } \\ & { = \left( \begin{array} { l l } { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } \left( r _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( r _ { 2 } + s \right) + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } \left( w _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( w _ { 2 } + s \right) } { \left( u _ { O F F } + s \right) \left( u _ { O N } + s \right) \left( w _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( w _ { 2 } + s \right) } } \\ { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } \left( r _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( r _ { 2 } + s \right) + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } \left( w _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( w _ { 2 } + s \right) } { \left( u _ { O F F } + s \right) \left( u _ { O N } + s \right) \left( w _ { 1 } + s \right) \left( w _ { 2 } + s \right) } } \end{array} \right) . } \end{array}

$$  

Denoting the roots of $\mathbb{C} \ni s \mapsto k_3^+k_3^-(r_1-s)(r_2-s) + k_2^+k_2^-(w_1-s)(w_2-s)$ by $q_1, q_2$ we rewrite  

$$

\tilde { \boldsymbol { q } } ^ { * } ( s ) = \left( \begin{array} { l l } { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { ( k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } ) ( q _ { 1 } + s ) ( q _ { 2 } + s ) } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) ( w _ { 1 } + s ) ( w _ { 2 } + s ) } } \\ { \frac { k _ { \mathrm { t x } } } { u _ { O N } + s } } & { \frac { ( k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } ) ( q _ { 1 } + s ) ( q _ { 2 } + s ) } { ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( u _ { O N } + s ) ( w _ { 1 } + s ) ( w _ { 2 } + s ) } } \end{array} \right) .

$$  

For the last filtering step we use that $\frac{a}{b}\frac{1}{1-\frac{a}{b}}=\frac{a}{b-a}$ and $1+\frac{a}{b-a}=\frac{b}{b-a}$ for any $a\neq b$. We obtain  

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { f _ { \tau } ^ { * } ( s ) = \big \{ k _ { 1 x } ( u _ { O F F } + s ) ( w _ { 1 } + s ) ( w _ { 2 } + s ) \big \} } \\ & { \qquad \times \left\{ u _ { O F F } + s \right) ( u _ { O N } + s ) ( w _ { 1 } + s ) ( w _ { 2 } + s ) } \\ & { \qquad - \left( k _ { 3 } ^ { + } k _ { 3 } ^ { - } + k _ { 2 } ^ { + } k _ { 2 } ^ { - } \right) ( q _ { 1 } + s ) ( q _ { 2 } + s ) \big \} ^ { - 1 } . } \end{array}

$$  

Denote $R(s):=k_{\mathrm{tx}}(u_{OFF}+s)(w_{1}+s)(w_{2}+s)$ and let $v_{1},\ldots,v_{4}$ be the roots of $U(s):=(u_{OFF}+s)(u_{ON}+s)(w_{1}+s)(w_{2}+s)-(k_{3}^{+}k_{3}^{-}+k_{2}^{+}k_{2}^{-})(q_{1}+s)(q_{2}+s)$. Then  

$$

f _ { \tau } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { 4 } \frac { R ( v _ { k } ) } { U ^ { \prime } ( v _ { k } ) } e ^ { v _ { k } t } .

$$

[96] To provide intuition, we'll briefly outline the idea behind information units, using the bit as an example. A bit is the unit  

of information that can be stored (capacity) in a data storage device with two (equally addressable) states (i.e., no state is a priori more likely than the other). That is, any state of this storage device provides 1 bit of information. Consequently, a data storage device with $N$ equally addressable states can store $\log_2(N)$ bits of information (which equals the number of binary storage units required to achieve the same capacity). Shannon extended this notion to the information content of data or signals. For a message $M \sim p(m)$ with discrete alphabet $\mathcal{M}$ the symbol $m$ contains $I(m) := -\log_2(p(m))$ bits of information. Now, $I(m)$ is the number of equally addressable binary information units required to represent the same amount of information. That is, we would be equally surprised to find this state in a storage device of $I(m)$ bits, if all states were equally likely. in this sense, $H(M)/t = \sum_m p(m)I(m)/t$ is the mean rate of information of $M$. The inequality in (55) is a standard result [110].
